# CAP. V. de causa vocabuli, dum rei causa idololatria sit.

Nam et cum promiscue^5 ludi Liberalia vocarentur, honorem Liberi patris manifeste sonabant. Libero enim a rusticis primo fiebant ob beneficium quod ei ascribunt, demonstrata gratia vini. Exinde ludi Consualia dicti qui initio Neptunum honorabant. Eundem enim et Consum^6 vocant. Dehinc Equiria^7 Marti Romulus dixit, quamquam et Consualia Romulo defendunt^8, quod ea Conso dicaverit Deo, ut volunt, consilii, ejus scilicet, quo tunc Sabinarum virginum rapinam militibus suis in matrimonia excogitavit. Probum plane consilium^9 et nunc quoque inter ipsos Romanos justum et licitum, ne dixerim, penes Deum. Facit enim et hoc ad originis maculam, ne bonum existimes quod initium a malo accepit, ab impudentia violentiæ, ab alio deo^10, a fratricida institutore, a filio Martis. Et nunc ara Conso illi in Circo defossa est ad primas metas sub terra, cum inscriptione hujusmodi :

->CONSUS CONSILIO MARS DUELLO LARES COILLO^11 POTENTES.<-

Sacrificant

---

for the origin of the term, so long as the origin of the thing is idolatry.' If the Lupercalia were called *games* on account of the sports, the sports being consecrated to religious service proves my point.

^5 *promiscue*. La Cerda notes the mistake of those who conceive Tertullian to say that the name *Liberalia* was given indifferently to all games. Dodgson falls into this error. The argument is this. Tertullian wishes to shew that the origin of the games is idolatrous, whatever be the origin of the term 'games.' For, says he, the Liberalia, even when

<column-break/>

called by the common appellation of 'games,' clearly sounded the honor of Liber. For they were first established for his praise. Then came games called Consualia, and these were from the first in honor of Neptune. Next Romulus established the Equiria in honor of Mars.

^6 *Consum*, Liv. I. 9.

^7 *Equiria*. Equestrian games, celebrated in the Campus Martius. Ov. Fast. II. 859.

^8 *defendunt*, 'they claim the Consualia too for Romulus.' See below, c. 8, n. II.

^9 *probum plane*. Ironical. 'An

<margin>CAP. V.</margin>
apud eam Nonis Juliis sacerdotes publici, XII. Kalend. Septembres flamen Quirinalis et Virgines. Dehinc idem Romulus Jovi Feretrio ludos instituit in Tarpeio, quos Tarpeios dictos et Capitolinos Piso ^12^ tradidit. Post hunc Numa Pompilius Marti et Robigini fecit (nam et robiginis deam ^13^ finxerunt), dehinc Tullus Hostilius, dehinc Ancus Martius et ceteri quoque per ordinem. Et quibus idolis ludos instituerint, positum est apud Suetonium Tranquillum, vel a quibus Tranquillus accepit. Sed hæc satis erunt ad originis de idololatria reatum.

<margin>CAP. VI.</margin>
<margin>The titles of Games, whether national or funereal, are idolatrous.</margin>
Accedit ad testimonium antiquitatis subsecuta posteritas, formam originis de titulis hujus quoque temporis præferens, per quos signatum est, cui idolo et cui superstitioni utriusque generis ^1^ ludi notarentur. Megalenses enim et Apollinares, idem Cereales et Neptunales et Latiares et Florales in commune celebrantur; reliqui ludorum de natalibus et solemnitatibus regum et publicis prosperitatibus et municipalibus festis superstitionis causas originis

excellent device, forsooth, and one which is right and lawful among the Romans themselves, not to say before God!'

^10^ *ab impudentia...deo.* I retain the ancient reading. Oehl. follows Rigalt, who adopted a correction of Ursinus, *ab impudentia, a violentia, ab odio.* Cod. Ag. reads *ab impudentia a violentiæ*, where the insertion of *a* was probably an oversight. *Alio deo*, i. e. other than Consus, another so-styled god.

^11^ *Coillo. Cod. Ag. Coillo.* Gang. *Cum illo.* Gel. and Pam. *Comitio.* The last is probably only a conjec-

<column-break/>

ture, and as the Lares had nothing to do with the Comitium, not a very happy one. Rigalt conceives *coillum* to be *domus penetrale*, from *κοῖλον*, and that from *coillum* came *inquilinus* or *incoillinus*. Salmas. conjectures *colio*; *colium* from *colo*. The reading is doubtful, and no explanation satisfactory.

^12^ Piso. Arnob. III. p. 122 (Lug. Bat. 1651); Lact. D. I. I. 6.

habent. Inter quos etiam privatorum memoriis legatariæ editiones<gloss>2</gloss> parentant, id quoque secundum institutionis antiquitatem. Nam et a primordio bifariam ludi censebantur, sacri et funebres, id est deis nationum et mortuis. Sed de idololatria nihil differt apud nos, sub quo nomine et titulo, dum ad eosdem spiritus perveniat, quibus renuntiamus, licet mortuis, licet diis suis faciant. Proinde mortuis suis ut diis faciant<gloss>3</gloss>, una conditio partis utriusque est, una idololatria, una renuntiatio nostra adversus idololatriam.

<gloss>2</gloss> *legatariæ editiones*, the celebration of funeral games according to a testamentary disposition. See Lips. Saturn. i. c. 7. Lips. quotes Senec. De Brev. Ch. VII. <gloss>1</gloss> *pompa.* Ovid. Fast. iv. 391: *Circus erat pompa celeber, numeroque Deorum.* The *pompa Circensis* was a procession of those who were about to take part in the Games. It started from the temple of Jupiter Capitolinus, went round the Forum through the Velabrum to the Circus Maximus. The order was as follows: 1 the chief magistrates; 2 noble youths; 3 chariots and athletes; 4 saltatio armata; 5 saltatio satyrica; 6 tubicines and tibicines; 7 ministri sacrorum majorum; 8 ministri sacrorum minorum; 9 images of the gods or of the emperors on tensæ or fercula; 10 armamaxæ; 11 collegia augurum, &c. On arriving at the Circus sacrifices were offered to the various deities of the spina (see below, c. 8). This procession was

¹ at first peculiar to the Ludi Magni, instituted by **A. Postumius** after the battle of Regillus. Afterwards it was adopted generally at all Games.

² *probans*. **Rig.** from conjec. *probat*. This is unnecessary. The subst. verb *est* is understood, *paullo pompatior (est) suggestus*.

³ *de curribus, de tensis, de arma-maxis*. *currus*, the chariots which contended in the race; *tensæ* (from *tendo*), chariots containing images of gods drawn by ropes; *armamaxæ*, large wagons, on which arms were piled up as trophies. **Sueton.** 76 **Jul.** : *Ampliora etiam humano fastigio decerni sibi passus et sedem auream in curia, et pro tribunali tensam, et ferculum Circensi pompa, templa, aras, simulacra juxta Deos*.

⁴ *de sedibus, de coronis, de exuviis*. *sedes* or *sellæ* the thrones on which

<column-break/>

the images were placed; *exuviæ*, the dresses and other trappings of the gods. **De Coron.** c. 7: *de exuviis utriusque privigni*. **Oehler** quotes **Apul. Met.** XI. c. 10: *Sed antistites sacrorum proceres illi... potentissimorum deorum præferebant exuvias*.

⁵ *dæmoniorum conventus*. Comp. below, c. 12, *Capitolium omnium dæmonum templum est*; **Apolog.** c. 6, *Capitolio prohibitos, id est curia deorum pulsos*.

⁶ *quod*, ‘no matter whether there be display or economy in that which offends God.’ On *viderit* see **De Cor.** c. 2, n. 2. **Cod. Ag. sit. Gel.** and **Pamel.** *quo*. **Barræus** *quod*. **Rig.** *Viderit ambitio sive frugalitas ejus. Deum*. **Oehl.** from conject. *si*. **Oehler** does not mention the readings *quo* and *quod*, but says *quos* is the common reading.

⁷ *censu criminis sui.* Dodgs., ‘in the amount of its sinfulness.’ Oehl. explains *censu* by *origine*; but see De Idol. c. 4, n. 4.

Ch. VIII. ¹ On the Circus Maximus, see Smith’s Dict. of Greek and Rom. Antiq.

² *Soli.* Tac. Annal. xv. 74: *Tum decreta dona et grates Deis decernuntur, propriusque honos Soli, (cui est vetus ædes apud Circum, in quo facinus parabatur) qui multa conjurationum numine retexisset.*

³ *quod.* Cod. Ag. *qui*, adopted by Rig. and Oehl. The latter by conject. alters *habent* to *atque*. The reading of the text, which is that of the other Edd. is perfectly intelligible without alteration. The verbs *habent* and *affirmant* are used by Tertullian with a general nominative, such as *quidam*, understood. So throughout this chapter, *vovent*, *sustinent*, &c. and elsewhere.

⁴ *ova.* Livy, in a very corrupt passage (XLI. 32), says that Q. Fulvius Flaccus and A. Postumius Albinus the censors (A.U.C. 680) caused to be placed *ova ad notas curriculis numerandis*. This passage has been variously amended. At any rate it

<column-break/>

points to the adoption of *ova* to number the courses. But this is directly opposed to Dion Cassius (Lib. XLIX. p. 417 B. [Hanov. 1606]), who says of Agrippa, when Ædile (A.U.C. 721): κἀν τῳ ἱπποδρόμῳ σφαλλομένους τοὺς ἀνθρώπους περὶ τὸν τῶν διαύλων ἀριθμὸν τούς τε δελφῖνας καὶ τὰ ὠοειδῆ δημιουργήματα κατεστήσατο. Cassiodor. Var. III. 51: *Nec illud putetur irritum, quod metarum circuitus ovorum erectionibus exprimitur, quando actus ipse, multis superstitionibus gravidus, ovi exemplo geniturum se aliqua profitetur.* See notes in Drakenborch’s Livy, l. c. Panvinius draws a distinction between *ova Castorum* surmounting the *metæ*, and *ova curriculorum* on the *spina*. Ancient coins shew *ova* both on the *metæ* and on the *spina*. Is it not possible that *ova* were first erected on the *metæ* in 680, and afterwards on the *spina* in 721?

*vomunt. Columnæ*, making *Delphines* the nomin. The reading of the text (that of Gelen. and Pamel.) is preferable to Rigalt's, in which *vomunt columnæ* is very harsh, and has more authority than Oehler's. *Vovent*, 'men dedicate;' *sustinent*, 'men tolerate,' as Apol. c. 15, *sustinetis Jovis elogia cantari*. Perhaps there is some play on the word, as used in connexion with *columnas*.

⁷ *Sessias, Messias, Tutulinas*. Plin. N. H. XVIII. 2: *Seiamque a serendo, Segestam a segetibus appellabant, quarum simulacra in Circo videmus*. Macrob. Saturn. I. 16: *apud veteres quoque qui nominasset Salutem, Senoniam, Seiam, Segetiam, Tutilinam ferias observabat*. Comp. Apolog. c. 52; De Idol. c. 15; De Cor. c. 13. Arnob. adv. Gent. III. p. 115: *Unxionibus, inquit, superest Unxia, cingulorum Cinxia replicationi, Victa et Potua sanctissimæ victui potuique procurant. O egregia numinum et singularis interpretatio potestatum! nisi postes virorum adipali unguine oblinerentur a sponsis, nisi virginalia vincula jam furentes dissolverent atque imminentes mariti, nisi potarent et manderent*

<column-break/>

*homines, Dii nomina non haberent!* S. Augustin (C. D. iv. 8) enlarges on such deities as *Segetia, Tutilina, Runcina*, &c. Luther (Op. Tom. I. p. 8 [Witteb. 1582]) compares the popular saints with these pagan deities: *Adhuc non pudet nos Christianos ita in sanctos partiri negotia rerum temporalium, ac si essent nunc facti servi et mancipia artificum; ita ut prope redierit ea Lerna superstitionum, ut rursus Romanorum illud chaos deorum et quoddam Pantheon exstruxerimus*. Polytheism always appears in much the same shape. 'The Chinese,' says the Bishop of Victoria, in an account of a late tour, 'have peopled heaven, earth, and sea, and even the elements, with myriads of *Shin*, inferior divinities, the angels, ministers, and subordinate agents of the Supreme Being, *Shang-ta*.' He mentions *Tow-shin*, the god of the small-pox. It is found that the Chinese are too ready, when converted by Romanist missionaries, to adopt saint-worship in the place of Shin-worship.

⁸ *Magnis, Potentibus, Valentibus*. Macrob. Saturn. III. 4. Cassius Hemina dicit Samothracas deos eos-

### CAP. VIII.
Obelisci enormitas⁹, ut Hermateles affirmat, Soli prostituta; scriptura ejus, unde et census, de Ægypto superstitio est. Frigebat dæmonum concilium sine sua Matre Magna; ea itaque illic præsidet euripo¹⁰. Consus, ut diximus, apud metas sub terra delitescit¹¹. Murtias¹² quoque idolum fecit. Murtiam enim deam amoris volunt, cui in illa parte ædem vovere. Animadverte, Christiane, quot nomina immunda possederint Circum. Aliena est tibi religio, quam tot diaboli spiritus occupaverunt. De locis quidem locus est retractandi ad præveniendam quorundam interrogationem. Quid enim, inquis, si alio in tempore Circum adiero? periclitabor de inquinamento? Nulla est præscriptio de locis. Nam non sola ista conciliabula spectaculorum, sed etiam templa ipsa sine periculo disciplinæ adire servus Dei potest urgente causa simplici duntaxat, quæ non pertineat ad proprium ejus loci negotium vel officium. Ceterum et plateæ et forum et balneæ et stabula et ipsæ domus nostræ sine

---

demque Romanorum Penates dici, θεοὺς μεγάλους, θεοὺς χρηστούς, θεοὺς δυνατούς.' Oehl.

⁹ *obelisci enormitas*. An Egyptian obelisk, 125¾ feet high, besides the base, cut out of one stone was placed by Augustus in the Circus Maximus, and dedicated to the sun. Plin. N. H. xxxvi. 14, 5. The Hippodrome of Constantinople too had its obelisk, a very large one also from Egypt.

¹⁰ *Euripo*. Euripi were fosses round the Circus, originally made by Julius Cæsar, to protect the spectators from the wild beasts in the arena. Plin. N. H. viii. 7. Dodg-

<column-break/>

son considers the *euripus* to be an artificial lake for sea-fights, but it does not appear that there was ever such a lake in the Circus. Sueton. *Jul.* c. 39, mentions such a lake in *minore Codeta*, which was in the Campus Martius; and Dion Cass. quoted by Lipsius (Amph. p. 40) says that Nero exhibited naval fights in the amphitheatre. Lipsius quotes in the margin Senec. *Ep. 91, qui euripos subito aquarum impetu implent aut siccent*, which, perhaps, led to the confusion between the *euripi* and the artificial lakes.

Erichthonius, Minervæ et Vulcani filius, et quidem de caduca in terram libidine, portentum est dæmonicum, immo diabolus ipse, non coluber. Si vero Trochilus$^2$ Argivus auctor est currus, primæ Junoni id opus suum dedicavit. Si Romæ Romulus quadrigam primus ostendit, puto et ipse inter idola conscriptus est, si idem est Quirinus. Talibus auctoribus quadrigæ productæ merito et aurigas coloribus idololatriæ vestierunt. Namque initio duo soli$^3$ fuerunt, albus et russeus. Albus hiemi ob nives candidas, russeus æstati ob solis ruborem voti erant. Sed postea tam voluptate quam superstitione provecta russeum alii Marti, alii album Zephyris consecraverunt, prasinum vero Terræ Matri vel verno, venetum Cœlo et Mari vel autumno.

$^2$ *Trochilus*. Cod. Ag. *Prophylus*. Gangn. and Gel. *Acrophilus*. Pam., Rig. and Oehl. *Trochilus*. Oehl. say that Jerome ascribed the invention of the chariot both to Erichthonius and to Trochilus.

$^3$ *Namque initio*. Cod. Ag. *Nam æqui initio*. Rig. *Nam equi initio*. Gangn. *Nam initio equi*. Gel. and Pam. *Et ab initio duo soli*. The reading in the text is a conjecture of Salmasius. *Equi* is better omitted; *duo soli* sc. *colores*. These colours were adopted by parties with the utmost vehemence, *factio prasina*, *factio russea*; and there were in the Circus certain heads of the party, *domini factionis*. See Sueton. Ner. c. 5. Martial, Ep. x. 48, 23, alludes to the danger of

<margin>CAP. IX.</margin>
Cum autem omnis species idololatriæ damnata sit a Deo, utique etiam illa damnatur quæ elementis mundialibus profanatur.

<margin>CAP. X.</margin>
<margin>Of stage-plays both the place and art are idolatrous.</margin>
Transeamus ad scenicas res, quarum et originem communem et titulos pares secundum ipsam ab initio ludorum appellationem et administrationem conjunctam cum re equestri jam ostendimus. Apparatus autem ex ea parte consortes, qua ad scenam a templis et aris ab illa infelicitate thuris et sanguinis<sup>1</sup> inter tibias et tubas itur, duobus inquinatissimis arbitris funerum et sacrorum, designatore<sup>2</sup> et haruspice. Ita cum de originibus ludorum ad Circenses transiimus<sup>3</sup>, inde nunc ad scenicos ludos dirigimus<sup>4</sup> a loci vitio. Theatrum proprie sacrarium Veneris est. Hoc denique modo id genus operis in sæculo evasit. Nam sæpe censores<sup>5</sup> renascentia cum maxime theatra destruebant, moribus consulentes, quorum scilicet periculum ingens de <meta>catchword: de</meta>

---

sion; but funeral processions being most common, he became to be considered as especially belonging to funerals. So the *haruspex* was concerned with the offices of the dead. *Apol. c. 13: Nam et haruspex mortuis apparet.*

<sup>3</sup> *transiimus.* This is a conjecture of Junius adopted by Oehler, and previously by La Cerda. The ancient reading is *transimus*. Above *c. 9, l. 4*. Gangn. and Gelen. read *transit*, where Pamel. from the Cod. Clement. has *transiit*. 'Since from the origins of Games we made a transition to the Circensian, we now direct our course to theatrical shows, starting from the corruptness of the locality.' Tert. had discussed the origins and titles of Games both

<column-break/>

of the Circus and Theatre, (*c. 5, 6*). When he came to equipment, he was led especially to the Circus, and confined himself entirely to the Circus in the question of place and art. He now therefore, after again briefly touching on the equipment, considers the place and art of theatrical shows.

<sup>4</sup> *dirigimus.* De Jejun. *c. 11: Propterea per singulas direximus jejunationum species.* A *loci vitio* is in all the MSS. Rig. from conjecture, *a loci initio*; and Oehler, *a loci. Initio.*

<sup>5</sup> *censores. Apol. c. 6, quæ theatra stuprandis moribus orientia statim (censores) destruebant?* where Oehl. refers to passages mentioning such destruction.

CAP. X. Immo, quod ad artes pertinet, altius præscripsisse debemus<sup>15</sup>, dæmonas ab initio prospicientes<sup>16</sup> sibi inter cetera idololatriæ etiam spectaculorum inquinamenta, quibus hominem a Domino avocarent et suo honori obligarent, ejusmodi quoque artium ingenia inspirasse. Neque enim ab aliis procuratum fuisset quod ad illos perventurum esset: nec per alios tunc homines edidissent, quam per ipsos, in quorum nominibus et imaginibus et historiis fallaciam consecrationis sibi negotium acturæ constituerant<sup>17</sup>.

<margin>The same is the case as to the origin, titles, equipment, place, and art of the Stadium:</margin> CAP. XI. Ut ordo peragatur, ineamus etiam agonum<sup>1</sup>

<sup>15</sup> *altius præscripsisse debemus.* Dodgs.: 'we ought to have taken our rule from an earlier source, and to have said, that the dæmons, &c.' For the use of the infin. perfect comp. Lucret. i. 233: *Omnia enim debet, mortali corpore quæ sunt, Infinita ætas consumse.* Oehl. ad Scorp. c. 5, remarks Tertullian's fondness for perf. infinitives, quoting De Fug. c. 6, *poterant proposuisse.* ibid. c. 12, *maluit evitasse.* Apol. c. 2, *malunt credidisse.*

<sup>16</sup> *prospicientes.* Comp. De Cor. c. 7: *Si enim mendacium divinitatis diabolus operatur in hac etiam specie, a primordio mendax, sine dubio et ipse eos prospexerat, in quibus mendacium divinitatis ageretur.*

<sup>17</sup> *constituerant.* Cod. Ag. and Gangn. *constituerunt.* Pamel. ex cod. Clem. *constituerant*, followed by Rig. 'for neither would others have provided what was to come home to *them* (the dæmons); nor would they at that time have brought the arts into the world through the agency of other men, than of those very persons, in whose names, images

<column-break/>

and histories, they had contrived the cheat of a consecration.' On the consecration of images see De Idol. c. 15, n. 8.

CH. XI. <sup>1</sup> *agonum.* Tert. briefly considers agones under the heads of *origo*, *tituli*, *apparatus*, *locus*, *artes*. *Agones* were properly in Greece what *ludi* were in Rome, and they had a common origin. But although Tarquinius Priscus is said to have introduced pugiles in the Circus, the only Games which became truly national at Rome were the chariot-races. C. Fulvius first introduced athletes after the Ætolian war 186 B.C.; and in later days Greek Games became fashionable under the name of *Agones*. Scorp. c. 6: *Agonas istos contentiosa solemnia et superstitiosa certamina Græcarum et religionum et voluptatum quanta gratia sæculum celebret etiam Africæ liquuit.* There was a *Circus Agonalis* at Rome, and Panvinius engraves a coin representing Aurelian sacrificing at the *metæ*, inscribed *Agonalia Aug. Aureliani Augusti.*

retractatum. Origo istis de ludorum propinquitate

---

<columns>2</columns>
² *de collegiariis ministris*, servants of the various religious corporations—of Augures, of Pontifices, of Quindecimviri, and the like. All the adjuncts here mentioned had place in the Circus as well as the stadium.

³ *ut de loco communi.* Cod. Ag. and Gangn. *at de loco.* Gel. and Pam. *ut.* Rig. ac. Jun. and Oehl. *et.* The latter especially warns the reader against connecting *et de loco communi* with *suppleam*. He refers it to the following clause, and considers the words *in circo* (probably a misprint for *in stadio*) as used by way of exegesis. The repetition of *de loco* seems to carry us back rather than forward, and as *et* and *ac* are both conjectural, and *at* is inadmissible, I have retained *ut*. 'To add a word in regard to a locality forming a kind of common ground for the arts of the Muses, of Minerva, of Apollo, and also of Mars; by battle and by trumpets they imitate the Circus in the sta-

<column-break/>

dium, which is even of itself, without such adjuncts, a temple of the idol whose solemnities it celebrates.'

⁴ *per duellum.* The Cod. Ag. omits these words. Dodgs. connects them with *Martialium*; but the battle and trumpets were both principal features in which the stadium resembled the Circus.

⁵ *in stadio.* Oehler reads *in stadio*. Cod. Ag. as collated by Baluz, *stadio*; more recently by Hildebrand *in statio*. Gangn. *aut stadio*. Gel. and Pamel. *ut stadio*, who also read *æmulentur*. The Ludi Martiales and Apollinares were usually celebrated in the Circus Maximus, but on extraordinary occasions a stadium was prepared elsewhere as for the athletic contests exhibited by Julius Cæsar. Sueton. Jul. c. 39. Cicero in his classification of public games does not mention the stadium, but assigns athletic exercises to the Circus. *De Leg. II. 15: Jam ludi publici quum sint cavea, circoque divisi, sint corporum exercitationes*,

CAP. XI. est⁶ et ipsum ejus idoli, cujus sollemnitates agit. Sed et gymnicas artes Castorum et Herculum et Mercuriorum disciplinæ prodiderunt.

<margin>
CAP. XII.
and of the Amphitheatre.
</margin>
Superest illius insignissimis spectaculi acceptissimi¹ recognitio. Munus² dictum est ab officio, quoniam officium etiam muneris nomen est. Officium autem mortuis hoc spectaculo facere se veteres arbitrabantur, posteaquam illud humaniore atrocitate³ temperaverunt. Nam olim, quoniam animas defunctorum humano sanguine propitiari creditum erat, captivos vel malo ingenio servos mercati in exsequiis immolabant. Postea placuit impietatem⁴

---

*cursu et pugillatione, luctatione curriculisque equorum usque ad certam victoriam, circo constituti; cavea cantu, voce ac fidibus et tibiis dummodo ea moderata sint, ut lege præscribitur.* The erection and use of stadia was more common under the emperors. The stadium was especially for athletic and musical contests, both of exotic growth. In the vast Thermæ built during the empire there was always a stadium for athletes. So Mart. Epig. iii. 68: *Gymnasium, Thermæ, stadium est hac parte; recede, Exuimur, nudos parce videre viros.*

⁶ *templum est.* The connexion of a stadium with idolatry was seen at Daphne in Syria. See Gibbon’s Decline and Fall, ch. xxiii.

Ch. XII. ¹ *insignissimis...acceptissimi.* Rig. proposes *insignissimi ac septissimi.* Oehl. from conj. *insignissimi, ac receptissimi.* No change is necessary.

² *munus.* Gladiatorial shows were not *ludi*, but *munera*. Suet. Jul. c. 39: *munere in foro depugnavit.* Apul. Met. iv. 13, gives

<column-break/>

an account of the preparations for a *munus*: *Gladiatores isti famosæ manus, venatores illi probatæ pernicitatis, alibi noxii perdita securitate suis epulis bestiarum saginas instruentes. Confixilis machina, publicæ turres tabularum nexibus ad instar circumforaneæ domus, floridæ picturæ, decora futuræ venationis receptacula. Qui præterea numerus, quæ species ferarum!*

³ *humaniore atrocitate*, 'with a more refined cruelty.' The Romans under Valentinian who exposed the captive Saxons to gladiatorial combats, and complained that twenty-nine had by suicide disappointed the people of their pleasure, were shocked at the human sacrifices of these Saxons. Gibb. c. xxv. Gladiators were first exhibited at Rome by M. and D. Junius Brutus in honor of their deceased father, A.U.C. 490. At first they were very rare and only shewn at public funerals (*indictiva*). But under the Emperors they became so common, that the people used to stop private funerals and demand such exhibitions of the

<margin>CAP. XII.</margin>
voluptate adumbrare. Itaque quos paraverant armis, quibus tunc et qualiter poterant eruditos tantum ut occidi discerent⁵, mox edicto die inferiarum apud tumulos erogabant⁶. Ita mortem homicidiis consolabantur. Hæc muneris origo. Sed paulatim provecti ad tantam gratiam, ad quantam et crudelitatem, quia ferarum⁷ voluptati satis non fiebat, nisi et feris humana corpora dissiparentur. Quod ergo mortuis litabatur, utique parentationi deputabatur. Quæ species proinde idololatria est, quoniam et idololatria parentationis est species⁸. Tam hæc quam illa mortuis ministrat. In mortuo-

heir. Tiberius had once to disperse by soldiers a mob collected in the forum for this purpose. Suet. Tib. c. 37.
⁴ *impietatem*. The amphitheatre was sacred to Jupiter Infernalis, or Latiaris, or Dis, on whose altar sacrifices were made. Apolog. c. 9: *Ecce in illa religiosissima Æneadarum urbe Jupiter quidam quem ludis suis humano sanguine proluunt. Sed bestiariorum, inquitis. Opinor hoc minas quam hominum.* Lipsius (de Amph. c. 3) quotes this, and conjectures *bestiarii, hominis*, considering that a particular man's blood was sprinkled as a sacrifice, quoting from Minuc. Fel. c. 30: *Hodieque a Romanis Latiaris Jupiter homicidio colitur, et, quod Saturni filio dignum est, mali et noxii hominis sanguine saginatur.* Comp. Prudent. adv. Symm. I. 396: *Funditur humanus Latiari in munere sanguis, Concessusque ille spectantum solvit ad aram Plutonis fera vota sui. Quid sanctius ara, Quæ bibit egestum per mystica tela cruorem.* Scorp. c. 7: *Et Latio ad hodiernum Jovi media in urbe sanguis ingustatur.* But the

<column-break/>

general effusion of blood is probably all that is meant in these passages.
⁵ *quibus tunc....* ‘instructed in the use of whatever weapons and in whatever way they could then be instructed only in order that they might learn to be killed.’ There were numerous schools (*ludi*) for gladiators at Rome. Lips. Saturn. I. c. 14.
⁶ *erogabant. Erogare*, ‘to expend, waste, consume.’ Ad Martyr. c. 4: *Quis ergo non libentissime habeat tantum erogare pro vero, quantum alii pro falso.* De Idol. c. 8: *coronas luxuria erogat.* There may be here some allusion to the *rogus*, or funeral pyre.
⁷ *ferarum*, ‘of beasts in human shape,’ men who are savage as wild beasts. Comp. Tit. i. 12: κακὰ θηρία.
⁸ *quod ergo....* Tertul. proves the munus to be idolatrous, thus: It originated in an offering to the dead, therefore it was a kind of *parentatio* (worship of the dead). But idolatry is itself a kind of *parentatio* (because it originates in the worship of the dead). Therefore the munus is idolatrous.

CAP. XII. rum autem idolis dæmonia consistunt. Ut et titulos consideremus, licet transierit hoc genus editionis ab honoribus mortuorum ad honores viventium, quæsturas⁹ dico et magistratus et flaminia et sacerdotia, cum tamen nominis dignitas¹⁰ idololatriæ crimine teneatur, necesse est, quicquid dignitatis nomine administratur, communicet etiam maculas ejus, a qua habet causas. Idem de apparatibus interpretabimur in ipsorum honorum suggestu deputandis, quod purpuræ, quod fasces, quod vittæ, quod coronæ, quod denique conciones et edicta¹¹, et pultes pridianæ¹², sine pompa diaboli, sine invitatione dæmonum non sunt. Quid ergo de horrido loco perorem, quem nec perjuria sustinent¹³? Pluribus enim et asperioribus nominibus amphitheatrum consecratur, quam Capitolium omnium dæmonum templum est¹⁴. Tot illic immundi spiritus considunt, quot homines capit. Ut de artibus concludam, Martem et Dianam¹⁵ utriusque ludi præsides novimus.

<margin>
C. XIII.
These idola-
</margin>
Satis, opinor, implevimus ordinem, quot et qui-

---

⁹ *quæsturas, &c.* alluding to the custom of magistrates exhibiting *munera* to celebrate their appointment. See Lips. Saturn. i. c. 9.

¹⁰ *nominis dignitas*. The dignity attaching to the name of *flamen* and *sacerdos* was plainly idolatrous. And Tertul. regards all heathen magistracies in the same light. De Idol. c. 18: *Nam dæmonia magistratus sunt sæculi*.

¹¹ *conciones et edicta*. The gladiatorial games were prefaced by harangues and edicts. Pamel. quotes Liv. ii. 24: *Concioni deinde edicto addidit fidem, quo edixit, ne quis civem Romanum vinctum aut clausum teneret.*

<column-break/>

¹² *pultes pridianæ*. Dodgs. considers this to allude to the banquets of the gladiators the night before the combat. See note 2 in this chapter, and below, c. 18, n. 3. More probably it refers to the custom of setting before the gods offerings of food and cakes. Arnob. vii. p. 235.

¹³ *quem nec perjuria sustinent*. A place too terrible even for the heathen to perjure themselves in. It was customary to take oaths in the Capitol; and Tertull. turns this into an argument for the local terrors of the amphitheatre, as if men were thereby deterred from swearing there and swearing falsely, which they scrupled not to do in the Capitol.

<margin>Shows are forbidden not only as being idolatrous, but also as included among worldly lusts;</margin>
Nunc interposito nomine¹ idololatriæ, quod solum subjectum sufficere deberet ad abdicationem spectaculorum, alia jam ratione tractemus ex abundanti, propter eos maxime qui sibi blandiuntur, quod non nominatim abstinentia ista præscripta sit², quasi parum etiam de spectaculis pronuntietur, cum <margin>Tit. ii. 12.</margin> concupiscentiæ sæculi damnantur. Nam sicut pecuniæ vel dignitatis vel gulæ vel libidinis vel gloriæ, ita et voluptatis concupiscentia est. Species autem voluptatis spectacula. Opinor, generaliter nominatæ concupiscentiæ continent in se et voluptates; æque generaliter intellectæ voluptates specialiter et in spectacula disseruntur.

Ch. XIV. ¹ *nomine*. Cod. Ag. and Gangn. *nosse ne idololatriæ*. Gel. *nosse ne idololatria*. Pamel. *nosse de idololatria*. Oehler: 'cum satis interim constet de nomine idololatriæ.' More probably, 'having placed the name of idolatry as a barrier between Christians and shows, I will proceed to give other reasons *ex abundanti*.'

Misit⁴, dicunt, et nuntiant invicem quod simul ab omnibus visum est. Teneo testimonium cæcitatis: non vident missum quid sit. Mappam putant, sed est diaboli ab alto præcipitati figura⁵. Ex eo itaque itur in furias et animos et discordias⁶, et quicquid non licet sacerdotibus pacis⁷. Inde maledicta, convicia sine justitia odii, etiam suffragia sine merito amoris. Quid enim suum consecuturi sunt qui illic agunt, qui sui non sunt? nisi forte hoc solum, per quod sui non sunt: de aliena infelicitate contristantur, de aliena felicitate lætantur. causa amare liceat, quam sine causa odisse. Deus certe etiam cum causa prohibet odisse, qui inimicos diligi jubet. Deus etiam cum causa maledicere non sinit, qui maledicentes benedici præcipit. Sed Circo quid amarius, ubi ne principibus quidem⁹ aut civibus suis parcunt ? Si quid horum, quibus Circus furit, alicubi competit sanctis, etiam in Circo licebit ; si vero nusquam, ideo nec in Circo.

<margin>
C. XVII.
The prohibition of impurity prohibits the impurities of the theatre.
Rom. xiii. 13.
</margin>
Similiter impudicitiam omnem amoliri¹ jubemur. Hoc igitur modo etiam a theatro separamur, quod est privatum consistorium impudicitiæ, ubi nihil probatur, quam quod alibi non probatur. Ita summa gratia ejus de spurcitia plurimum concinnata est, quam Atellanus gesticulator, quam mimus² etiam per mulieres repræsentat, sexum pudoris ex-

---

Gangn. *An forsitan.* Gel. and Pam. *Et forsitan.* Rig. *An fors sit, tam.* *Quam* is used with the ellipse of *magis* or *potius*. Tac. Annal. iv. 61: *claris majoribus, quam vetustis.* Tac. German. c. 6: *consilii quem formidinis arbitrantur.* Apul. Apolog. c. 28: *Idque a me susceptum officii gratia quam lucri causa docebo.* Similar to this is the ellipse of *magis* after *quanto*. Ad Natt. ii. 12: *quanto diffusa res est, tanto substringenda nobis erit.* On *tanto abest*, see De Cor. c. 14, n. 1. Comp. also the ellipse of *aliud* before *quam*. See De Cor. c. 14, n. 3.

⁹ *ne principibus quidem.* In Cassiod. Var. I. 27, there is a letter from Theodoric directing certain patricians and prefects not to injure *populum partis Prasini. Sed ne forsitan magnificos viros loquacitas popularis offenderit, præsumptionis hujus habenda descriptio est. Teneatur ad culpam, quisquis transeunti reveren-*

<column-break/>

*tissimo senatori injuriam inflixit si male optavit cum bene loqui debuit. Mores autem graves in spectaculis quis requirat? Ad Circum nesciunt convenire Catones. Quicquid illic gaudenti populo dicitur, injuria non putatur. Locus est qui defendit excessum, quorum garrulitas si patienter accipitur, ipsos quoque principes ornare monstratur. Respondeant nobis certe qui talibus studiis occupentur, si tranquillos optant adversarios suos. Certe volunt eos esse victores, qui ad injurias hic prosiliunt, cum se superatis turpiter erubescunt. Unde ergo irasci volunt, quod sine dubio se optasse cognoscunt?*

Ch. XVII. ¹ *impudicitiam amoliri.* So Gangn., Gel. and Pamel. Cod. Ag. and the margin of Gangn. have *nec impudicitiam amare*, which Rig. adopts, explaining *nec omnem* by *nullam*. The position of *nec* scarcely admits of this. Oehl. by conject. *amandare.*

---

² *mimus*. Gel. *minus*. Pamel. *mimus ex emend.*, which is adopted by Rig. Oehl. *minus*, omitting all mention of any various reading. He also reads without notice *dei spurcitia*. No edition that I have seen has *dei*; perhaps both *dei* and *minus* are misprints. Oehl. from Cod. Ag. and Gangn. *muliebres*, sc. *vestes*, referring to c. 23: *qui muliebribus vestietur*. Probably *muliebribus* is there neuter, from *muliebria*. The allusion is here to the indecencies of the Floralia. Comp. Lact. D. I. I. 20: *Exuuntur etiam vestibus populo flagitante meretrices, quæ tunc mimorum funguntur officio*.

³ *sexum pudoris exterminans*, ‘sexum extra terminos pudoris abducens,’ Rig. Comp. Apul. Met. III. 22, *exterminatus animi*, and ibid. v. 18, *extra terminum mentis suæ posita*.

⁴ *ut facilius domi...* ‘that they may be put to the blush at home more readily than upon the stage,’

<column-break/>

i. e. that they may do things openly upon the stage which they would blush to do even in the privacy of their homes.

⁵ *pantomimus*. Comp. Arnob. IV. p. 151: *Quid pantomimi vestri, quid histriones, quid illa mimorum atque exoleti generis multitudo?*

⁶ *erubescant*. The light in which even the heathen regarded these spectacles may be seen from the following law, *De repudiis*, quoted by Lips. Amph. c. 3:

> *Nec ullo modo uxorem expellat, nisi adulteram, vel Circensibus vel theatralibus vel arenariis spectaculis, se prohibente, gaudentem*.

See also a letter of Pliny, Ep. VII. 24. The Emperor Julian in a rescript for the reformation of the pagan religion, prescribes to all priests abstinence from the circus, theatre, and amphitheatre. Julian, Op. p. 555 (Paris, 1630). *Semel anno*, at the annual celebration of the Floralia.

$^7$ *communicant*, Gr. κοινοῖ, 'makes common or unclean.' Erasm. 'impurum facit.' Vulg. *Illa sunt quæ communicant hominem. De Patient. c. 8: Non vasculorum inquinamentis, sed eorum quæ ex ore promuntur, hominem communicari.*

Ch. XVIII. $^1$ *lusoriam vel agonisticam scenam dispungunt.* Heraldus on Arnob. III. p. 109, refers to the common use of ἀγωνισταὶ for actors, and explains *lusoriam vel agonisticam scenam*, as 'the comic and tragic stage.' Most commentators agree with Heraldus. But no

<column-break/>

authority is given for such a use of the words. Is not *lusoria scena* the common theatrical representations, including tragedy and comedy—the *agonistica*, such representations as were common in the *agones*, where there were trials of musical skill, and similar exhibitions? *dispungere*, 'to draw up accounts, setting one side against the other,' Apolog. c. 37, *malum malo dispungi*; Adv. The devil overthrew our first parents; therefore the art of throwing over is of the devil.

^5 ipse gestus... 'The very attitude of wrestling is as the coiling of a serpent.'

Sol, inquit, immo etiam ipse Deus de cælo spectat nec contaminatur. Sane sol et in cloacam radios suos defert, nec inquinatur. CAP. XX. nunquam excusator quod Deus damnat. Nusquam et nunquam licet quod semper et ubique non licet. Hæc est veritatis integritas et quæ ei debetur disciplinæ plenitudo et æqualitas timoris et fides obsequii, non immutare sententiam nec variare judicium. Non potest aliud esse quod vere quidem est bonum seu malum. Omnia autem penes veritatem Dei fixa sunt.

<margin>C. XXI.
The heathen gaze with delight in the Circus, Theatre, and Amphitheatre, on things at which they elsewhere shudder.</margin>
Ethnici, quos penes nulla est veritatis plenitudo, quia nec doctor veritatis Deus, malum ac bonum pro arbitrio ac libidine interpretantur, alibi bonum quod alibi malum, et alibi malum quod alibi bonum. Sic ergo evenit, ut qui in publico vix necessitate vesicæ tunicam levet, idem in Circo aliter non exsultet, nisi totum pudorem in faciem omnium intentet : ut et qui filiæ virginis ab omni spurco verbo aures tuetur, ipse eam in theatrum ad illas voces gesticulationesque deducat : et qui in plateis litem manu agentem aut compescit aut detestatur,

---

Ch. XXI. $^1$ *flagellis et virgis*. Lipsius (Saturn. II. c. 5) quotes from Senec. Ep. 7, the language of the infuriated populace: *Occide, ure, verbera. Quare tam timide incurrit in ferrum? quare parum audacter occidit? quare parum libenter moritur? Plagis aguntur in vulnera et mutuos ictus nudis et obviis pectoribus excipiunt.*

$^2$ *insigniori cuique homicidæ*. Apuleius (Met. x. 28) gives an account of a woman who had committed murder and other crimes: *Atque illam, minus quidem quam merebatur, sed quod dignus cruciatus alius excogitari non poterat, exserte bestiis objiciendam pronuntiavit.* Lact. D.

<column-break/>

I. VI. 20: *Nam qui hominem, quamvis ob merita damnatum, in conspectu suo jugulari pro voluptate computat, conscientiam suam polluit; tam scilicet, quem si homicidii, quod fit occulte, spectator et particeps fiat.*

$^3$ *rudem et pileum*. Mart. de Spect. 29: *Misit utrique rudes et palmas Cæsar utrique.*

$^4$ *illum vero...* 'Moreover it comes to pass, that he calls again for the gladiator who has received his death-blow, in order that he may gaze upon his face.' The savage delight of the people in scenes of blood is shewn by the mimic representation of the cruel death of Laureolus. Juv. Sat. VIII. 187 ; Mart. de Spect. 7.

etiam corpora sua substernunt, propter quos in ea committunt$^4$, quæ reprehendunt, ex eadem parte qua magnificiunt, deponunt et deminuunt, immo manifeste damnant$^5$ ignominia et capitis minutione$^6$, arcentes curia, rostris, senatu, equite, ceterisque honoribus omnibus simul et ornamentis quibusdam. **c. XXIII.** oculos Deus commendavit, ut vapulando deficiant. Taceo de illo, qui hominem leoni præ se opponit, ne parum sit homicida quam qui eundem postmodum jugulat⁹.

<margin>
**C. XXIV.**
It is clear then that these are the ‘pomps of the devil’ which we have renounced.
</margin>
**C. XXIV.** Quot adhuc perorabimus modis nihil ex his, quæ spectaculis deputantur, placitum Deo esse, aut congruens Dei servis quod Domino placitum non sit? Si omnia propter diabolum instituta et ex diaboli rebus instructa monstravimus (nihil enim non diaboli est, quicquid Dei non est vel Deo displicet), hoc erit pompa diaboli, adversus quam in signaculo fidei¹ ejeramus. Quod autem ejeramus, neque facto neque dicto neque visu neque prospectu participare debemus. Ceterum nonne ejeramus et rescindimus signaculum rescindendo testationem ejus? Numquid ergo superest, ut ab ipsis ethnicis responsum flagitemus? Illi nobis jam renuntient, an liceat Christianis spectaculo uti. Atquin hinc vel maxime² intelligunt factum Christianum de repudio spectaculorum. Itaque negat manifeste,

---

⁹ *Taceo de illo...* Rig. by conject. reads *qui* for *quam qui*, explaining it by saying that sometimes when the beasts were let in, one gladiator to save himself thrust another forward, who if successful against the lion sometimes fought with the first gladiator and was slain by him, who was thus twice a murderer. This is approved by Oehler, but seems to me forced. The reading of the MSS. *quam qui* is adopted by La Cerda, who conceives *qui jugulat* to be the lion. But there is little probability in this. The opposition lies between

<column-break/>

the gladiator who, as far as in him lies, causes the death of another by thrusting him forward to the lion, and him who actually puts him to death. If after a gladiator had fought, the people willed his death, another person slew him (*jugulabat*). ‘I say nothing of the case of him who thrust forward a combatant to the lion to screen himself, lest he be thought less of a murderer, than he who afterwards cuts the same combatant’s throat.’ *parum*, *quam* is equivalent to *minus*, *quam*. See above, c. 16, n. 8.

Ch. XXIV. ¹ *signaculo fidei*,

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>45</page-num>
<header>DE SPECTACULIS.</header>

<margin>c. XXIV.</margin>
qui, per quod agnoscitur, tollit. Quid autem spei superest in hujusmodi homine? Nemo in castra hostium transit, nisi projectis armis suis, nisi destitutis signis et sacramentis principis sui, nisi pactus simul perire.

<margin>
c. XXV.
Presence at these shows is incompatible with prayer and meditation.
</margin>
An ille recogitabit eo tempore de Deo, positus illic ubi nihil est de Deo? Pacem, opinor, habebit in animo contendens pro auriga, pudicitiam ediscet attonitus in mimos. Immo in omni spectaculo nullum magis scandalum occurret, quem ipse ille mulierum et virorum accuratior cultus. Ipsa consensio, ipsa in favoribus¹ aut conspiratio aut dissensio inter se de commercio scintillas libidinum conflabellant. Nemo denique in spectaculo ineundo prius cogitat, nisi videri et videre. Sed tragœdo vociferante exclamationes ille alicujus prophetæ retractabit? et inter effeminati histrionis modos psalmum secum comminiscetur? et cum athletæ agent, ille dicturus est repercutiendum non esse? <margin>Matt. v. 39.</margin> poterit et de misericordia moveri defixus in morsus ursorum et spongias² retiariorum? by gladiators to wipe off the blood from their wounds, also to clear the blood from the ground. Seneca (Ep. 70) gives an account of a gladiator who committed suicide by forcing such a sponge, affixed as usual to a stick, down his throat. Mart. Epig. XII. 48, *infelix damnatæ spongia virgæ*. Lips. (Saturn. II. c. 8) considers the sponges to be used as a covering to the breast. Liv. IX. 40, *spongia pectori tegumentum*. The contrast being *misericordia*, I take the allusion to be the first-named use of sponges.

We ought to avoid these hotbeds of corruption, even though the devil seasons his poison with some show of excellence.</margin> Odisse debemus istos conventus et cœtus ethnicorum, vel quod illic nomen Dei blasphematur, illic quotidiani in nos leones expostulantur, inde persecutiones decernuntur, inde tentationes emittuntur. Ch. XXIX. ¹ *retro*, 'in time past.' *Apolog. c. 1, neminem pœnitet nisi plane retro non fuisse. Ibid. c. 3, quos retro noverant, and retro patiens. Ibid. c. 19, retrosiores. Idol. c. 3, aliquamdiu retro.*

² *dæmonia expellis. Apol. c. 23: Edatur hic aliquis sub tribunalibus vestris, quem dæmone agi constet. Jussus a quolibet Christiano loqui spiritus ille tam se dæmonem confitebitur de vero, quam alibi deum de falso.* The power over demons,

<column-break/>

together with extraordinary revelations, is mentioned by Arnobius as common to all Christians. Arnob. I. p. 27: *qui [Christus] justissimis viris etiamnunc impollutis ac diligentibus sese, non per vana insomnia, sed per puræ speciem simplicitatis, apparet ; cujus nomen auditum fugat noxios spiritus.*

³ *revelationes petis.* The allusion to revelations and the new Jerusalem has been thought to savour of Montanism. Kaye's Tertul. p. 55.

<header>DE SPECTACULIS.</header>

<margin>CAP. XXIX.</margin>
delectant, satis nobis litterarum est, satis versuum est, satis sententiarum, satis etiam canticorum, satis vocum, nec fabulæ, sed veritates, nec strophæ⁵, sed simplicitates. Vis et pugillatus et luctatus? Præsto sunt non parva, sed multa. Aspice impudicitiam detectam a castitate, perfidiam cæsam a fide, sævitiam a misericordia contusam, petulantiam a modestia obumbratam, et tales sunt apud nos agones, in quibus ipsi coronamur. Vis autem et sanguinis aliquid? Habes Christi.

<margin>CAP. XXX.</margin>
<margin>The day of judgement shall afford us the most glorious spectacle in the punishment of the wicked, and the reward of the good. Apoc. v. 10, & xx. 5. Apoc. xxi. 2. 2 Pet. iii. 10.</margin>
Quale autem spectaculum in proximo est adventus Domini jam indubitati, jam superbi, jam triumphantis! quæ illa exsultatio angelorum, quæ gloria resurgentium sanctorum! quale regnum exinde justorum! qualis civitas nova Hierusalem! At enim supersunt alia spectacula, ille ultimus et perpetuus judicii dies, ille nationibus insperatus, ille derisus, cum tanta sæculi vetustas et tot ejus nativitates¹ uno igni haurientur. Quæ tunc spectaculi latitudo! quid admirer? quid videam? ubi gaudeam? ubi exsultem, spectans tot ac tantos reges, qui in cœlum recepti nuntiabantur, cum ipso Jove et ipsis suis testibus in imis tenebris congemiscentes! item præ-

---

<columns>2</columns>
But the topics are common to all Christians, and it is likely that they may have taken early hold on Tertullian's mind.

⁴ *Circenses ludos.* Observe the terms *cursus, metas, spatia, societates (factiones), signum (mappa), tuba, palma.*

⁵ *strophæ.* 'Sunt technæ et fraudes, quales in fabulis mimicis. Ad Natt. i. 10, *deos vestros in strophis et jocis rideatis.* Apol. c. 15, *utrum mimos an deos vestros in stro-*

<column-break/>

*phis et jocis derideatis. De Anima,* c. 28, *qui talem commentus est stropham.*' OEHL.

Ch. XXX. ¹ *tot ejus nativitates.* Dodgs. 'all that are rising into life.' More probably allusion is made to the Platonic notion of successive mundane revolutions. See Plat. Timæ. p. 22, sqq. For the use of *nativitas*, comp. Arnob. i. p. 3: *Ipsi homines, quos per inhabitabiles oras terræ sparsit prima incipiensque nativitas.*

Tunc magis tragœdi audiendi, magis scilicet vocales in sua propria calamitate; tunc histriones cognoscendi, solutiores multo per ignem; tunc spectandus auriga in flammea rota totus rubens⁴; tunc xystici contemplandi non in gymnasiis, sed in igne jaculati, nisi quod ne tunc quidem illos velim visos, ut qui malim ad eos potius conspectum insatiabilem conferre, qui in Dominum desævierunt. Hic est ille, dicam, fabri aut quæstuariæ⁵ filius, sabbati destructor, Samarites, et dæmonium habens. ---

² *nihil ad Deum pertinere*. Lact. *D. I. III. 17*: *Irreligiosus audit* (de Epicuro) *Deos nihil curare*. Arnobius, who is in many points unsound, is constantly recurring to this heathen notion. Lactantius seems to have written his *De Ira* expressly to controvert the opinions of his master on this point.

³ *animas aut nullas*. See the question of the reunion of the soul to the body, argued *Apol. c. 48*.

⁴ *totus rubens*, alluding to the

<column-break/>

colours of the race. Observe the terms *vocales* (see above, *c. 25*), *solutiores* (*c. 10*), *rubens* (*c. 23*).

⁵ *fabri aut quæstuariæ*, alluding to the Jewish blasphemy as to our Lord’s birth. S. Jerome to Heliodorus, *Ep. XIV.* (quoted by Oehler): *Hic est ille operarii et quæstuariæ filius, hic qui matris gestatus sinu hominem Deus fugit in Ægyptum, hic vestitus coccino, hic sentibus coronatus, hic magus dæmonium habens et Samarites.*

<margin>CAP. XXX.</margin>
<margin>Matt. xxvii. 64, & xxviii. 13.</margin>
centes surripuerunt, ut resurrexisse dicatur, vel hortulanus detraxit, ne lactucæ suæ frequentia commeantium læderentur. Ut talia spectes^6, ut talibus exsultes, quis tibi prætor aut consul aut quæstor aut sacerdos de sua liberalitate præstabit? Et tamen hæc jam quodammodo habemus per fidem spiritu imaginante repræsentata. Ceterum qualia illa sunt, quæ nec oculus vidit nec auris audivit nec in cor hominis ascenderunt? Credo, Circo et utraque cavea^7 et omni stadio gratiora. <margin>1 Cor. ii. 9.</margin>

---

<columns>2</columns>

^6 *ut talia spectes.* We cannot defend the spirit in which Tertullian exults in the prospect of the destruction of the wicked. See a note in the Oxf. Trans. Minucius Felix (Octav. c. 37) has a much more Christian account of a Christian spectacle in the sufferings of martyrs for Christ. S. Augustine (Enarr. in Psalm. 39) reproves a Christian audience for taking delight in the popular shows, and contrasts the spectacula fit for a Christian. *Mira-*

<column-break/>

*cula hominum intuebatur, intendat mirabilia Dei. Multa fecit Dominus mirabilia sua, hæc respiciat.* The whole passage is worth consulting as shewing the habits of society, and the sentiments of Christians as to these points in the fourth century.

^7 *utraque cavea.* Theatre and amphitheatre. Gangn. *caula.* Lips. de Amph. p. 14, quotes the passage with *caula*, adding 'si vera lectio est.'

# TERTULLIANI LIBER

## DE IDOLOLATRIA.

---

<margin>CAP. I.</margin>
<margin>
Idolatry is the chief crime of mankind. It includes in it murder, theft, adultery, and the like; and every crime is dæmon-service, and therefore idolatry.
</margin>
**P**RINCIPALE crimen generis humani, summus sæculi reatus, tota causa judicii idololatria. Nam etsi suam speciem tenet unumquodque delictum, etsi suo quoque nomine judicio destinatur, in idololatriæ tamen crimine expungitur¹. Omitte titulos², operas recognosce, idololatres idem homicida est. Quæris quem occiderit? Si quid ad elogii ambitionem facit³, non extraneum, nec inimicum, sed seipsum. Quibus insidiis? erroris sui. Quo telo? offensa Dei. Quot plagis? quotquot idololatriis. Qui negat idololatrem perisse, is negabit idololatrem homicidium fecisse. Proinde adulterium et stuprum in eodem recognoscas; nam

---

Ch. I. ¹ *expungitur*, ‘is disposed of under the charge of idolatry.’ On *expungere*, see De Cor. c. 1, n. 4.
² *titulos*, ‘the special names of the several crimes;’ e. g. *homicidium*, *stuprum*, &c. La Cerda quotes Suet. Cal. c. 32: *Servum, ob detractam lectis argenteam laminam, carnifici confestim tradidit, ut manibus abscissis atque ante pectus a collo pendentibus, præcedente titulo, qui causam pœnæ indicaret, per cœtus circumduceretur.* Below, c. 11, *titulo idololatriæ*.
³ *Si quid ad elogii...* ‘if it helps to complete the charge, [I answer].’ *elogium*, ‘the summary of an accusation,’ De Cor. c. 5: *elogium sacri-*

<column-break/>

*legii in Deum*. De Jejun. c. 17: *Ad elogium gulæ tuæ pertinet, quod etc.* La Cerda quotes Sueton. Cal. c. 27: *Custodiarum seriem recognoscens, nullius inspecto elogio, stans tantummodo intra porticum mediam, a calvo ad calvum duci imperavit. Ambitio*, ‘the compass;’ De Jejun. c. 17, *ambitio corporis*; De Anim. c. 25, *tota ventris ambitio. Ambitio elogii* is ‘the accusation set out to its full extent,’ including all the particulars, quem? quibus insidiis? quo telo? quot plagis? Comp. Ad Natt. I. 2: *Porro de nobis, quos atrocioribus ac pluribus criminibus deputatis, breviora ac leviora elogia conficitis.*

<vocab>Tertullianus, De Idololatria, reatus, expungere, elogium, ambitio, homicidium, La Cerda, Suetonius</vocab>

⁴ *stupri vocabulo*. Comp. Ezek. c. xxiii ; Hos. c. ii. and iv ; and elsewhere.
⁵ *conditionem*. Adv. Marc. II. c. 22 : *longe diversas habendo causas ab idololatriæ conditione*. *Repræsentare*, a judicial term, ‘to bring into court ;’ hence, ‘to bring before us, whether to our senses or minds.’ Adv. Marc. IV. c. 22 : *Itaque jam repræsentans eum : Hic est filius meus*. De Pœn. c. 3 : *adeo quod prohibetur administrare, satis periculose animus sibi repræsentat*. ⁶ *mendacium*. Comp. Is. xliv. 20.

<vocab>Tertullianus, idololatria, adulterium, fraus, repræsentare, stupri vocabulo, mancipes</vocab>

<header>DE IDOLOLATRIA.</header>

libidinis causa frequententur. <margin>CAP. I.</margin> In illa injustitia. Quid enim injustius ea quæ justitiæ patrem nescit? In illa etiam vanitas, cum tota ejus ratio vana sit. In illa mendacium⁶, cum tota substantia mendax sit. Ita fit ut omnia in idololatria et in omnibus idololatria deprehendatur. Sed et alias, cum universa delicta adversus Deum sapiant, nihil autem quod adversus Deum sapiat, non dæmoniis et immundis spiritibus deputetur quibus idola mancipantur, sine dubio idololatriam admittit quicumque delinquit. Id enim facit quod ad idolorum mancipes⁷ pertinet.

<margin>CAP. II.</margin>
<margin>Idolatry consists not only in the acts of sacrificing, burning incense, and the like, but has a more extended application.</margin>
Sed universa¹ nomina criminum discedant in operum suorum proprietates, remaneat idololatria in eo quod ipsa est, sufficit sibi tam inimicum Deo nomen, tam locuples substantia criminis, quæ tot ramos porrigit, tot venas defundit², ut de hoc cum maxime materia suscepta sit, quot modis nobis præcavenda sit idololatriæ latitudo, quoniam multifariam servos Dei nec tantum ignorata, sed etiam dissimulata subvertit. Plerique idololatriam simpliciter existimant his solis modis interpretandam,

---

<columns>2</columns>
*De Spect. c. 10: Apollines mancipes habent. Ad Natt. II. 2: auctoritas philosophorum, ut mancipium sapientiæ. Comp. below, c. 13: humanæ superstitionis deis mancipatæ; and De Spect. c. 12: In mortuorum idolis dæmonia consistunt. See below, c. 15, n. 8.*

Ch. II. ¹ *Sed universa...* 'But let the general denominations of sins pass, and let them be arranged each under the special head denoted by its act; let idolatry be confined to that which is specially idolatry; there is enough in a name so hostile to God, in so extensive a ground

<column-break/>

for accusation, that puts forth so many branches, descends into so many channels, that from this head chiefly has my theme been taken up, to shew in how many ways we must take precautions against the whole extent of idolatry.'

² *defundit*. Rig. from conj. *diffundit*. La Cerda quotes S. Cypr. *De Patient. p. 253 (Ed. Bened.): Late patet Patientiæ virtus, et ubertas ejus et largitas de unius quidem nominis fonte proficiscitur, sed exundantibus venis per multa gloriarum itinera diffunditur.*

### CAP. II.
si quis aut incendat aut immolet aut polluceat³ aut sacris aliquibus aut sacerdotiis obligetur, quemadmodum si quis adulterium in osculis et in amplexibus et in ipsa carnis congressione censendum, aut homicidium in sola sanguinis profusione, et in animæ ereptione reputandum. <margin>
CAP. III.
---
atry is service performed to any image, whatever it may represent.
</margin>
latria agebatur, non in isto nomine, sed in isto opere. Nam et hodie extra templum et sine idolo agi potest. At ubi artifices statuarum et imaginum et omnis generis simulacrorum diabolus sæculo intulit, rude illud negotium humanæ calamitatis et nomen de idolis consecutum est et profectum. Exinde jam caput facta est idololatriæ ars omnis quæ idolum quoquo modo edit. Neque enim interest an plastes effingat, an cælator exsculpat, an phrygio detexat, quia nec de materia refert an gypso, an coloribus, an lapide, an ære, an argento, an filo formetur idolum. Quando enim et sine idolo idololatria fiat, utique, cum adest idolum, nihil interest, quale sit, qua de materia, qua de effigie, ne qui putet id solum idolum habendum, quod humana effigie sit consecratum. Ad hoc necessaria est vocabuli interpretatio. <term>Εἶδος</term> Græce formam sonat; ab eo per deminutionem <term>εἴδωλον</term> deductum æque apud nos formulam fecit. Igitur omnis forma vel formula¹ idolum se dici exposcit. Inde idololatria omnis circa omne idolum famulatus et servitus. Inde et omnis idoli artifex ejusdem et unius est criminis, nisi parum idololatriam populus admisit qui simulacrum vituli et non hominis sibi conse-<margin>Exod. xxxii. 8.</margin>cravit.

<margin>
CAP. IV.
---
God condemns both image-worshippers and image-makers.
</margin>
Idolum tam fieri quam coli Deus prohibet. Quanto præcedit, ut fiat quod coli possit, tanto prius est, ne fiat, si coli non licet¹. Propter hanc

---

*mula. Arnob. Adv. Gent. vi. init. p. 189: In hac enim consuestis parte crimen nobis maximum impietatis affigere, quod neque ædes sacras ad venerationis officia construamus, non*

<column-break/>

*Deorum alicujus simulacrum constituamus aut formam, etc. Herald. in Arnob. vi. p. 194, adduces numerous passages to the same effect.*
*Ch. IV. ¹ Quanto præcedit...*

### CAP. IV.

<margin>
Exod. xx. 4.
Lev. xxvi. 1.
Deut. v. 8.
</margin>
causam, ad eradicandam scilicet materiam idololatriae, lex divina proclamat, Ne feceritis idolum, et conjungens, Neque similitudinem eorum quae in caelo sunt et quae in terra et quae in mari, toto mundo ejusmodi artibus interdixit² servis Dei. Antecesserat Enoch³ praedicens omnia elementa, omnem mundi censum⁴, quae caelo, quae mari, quae terra continentur, in idololatriam versuros daemonas <margin>Jud. 6.</margin> et spiritus desertorum angelorum, ut pro Deo adversus Dominum consecrarentur. Omnia igitur colit humanus error praeter ipsum omnium conditorem. Eorum imagines idola, imaginum consecratio idololatria. Quicquid idololatria committit, in artificem quemcumque et cujuscumque idoli depu-

---

¹ ‘As a thing must first be made before it can be worshipped, so a thing must first be forbidden to be made, if it may not be worshipped.’ La Cerda has a curious note upon this passage: ‘Locus notatissimus, ut scias nunquam jure naturali prohibitas imagines veri Dei. Itaque ab omni aevo ab ipso Adamo potuit fieri imago Dei ut coleretur. Hujus sententiae laudandus Tertullianus hoc loco: paritatem enim temporis in utraque constituit. Sicut nullo aevo potuit fieri quod non potuit coli, ita omni aevo potuit fieri, quod potuit coli. An vero jure scripto id vetitum Judaeis, dissident doctores duo aevi hujus gravissimi: Vasquius affirmat prohibitum, Soarius negat; postremo haereo ego tantulus.’ A truly notable deduction from such a chapter as this!

² *toto mundo*, ‘absolutely.’ *interdixit*, with dat. of the person, and abl. of the thing. Cic. Ep. Fam. xi. Ep. 1: *Futurum puto, ut hostes*

<column-break/>

*judicemur, aut aqua et igni nobis interdicatur. De Cor. c. 7: nostro capiti coronamentis interdicunt.*

³ Enoch. Cod. Ag., Gangn. and Gel. *enim hoc*. Enoch was first read by Pamel. who is followed by Rig. and Oehl. The book of Enoch is quoted below, c. 15, and *De Cult. Foem.* i. 3, where Tertul. says that it was rejected by some, *quia nec in armarium Judaicum admittitur*. Tertul. accepts it on the grounds of its being quoted by Jude, and because we are told, *omnem scripturam aedificationi habitem divinitus inspirari*, a singular interpretation of 2 Tim. iii. 16. Origen and Jerome both mention it as of doubtful authority. It was, according to Abp. Laurence, a book extant among the Jews before our Lord's time. See note in Oxford Translation.

⁴ *omnem mundi censum*, ‘the whole order of the universe.’ It is common to interpret *census* by *origo*, and *censeri* by *originem habere*, in

Apolog. c. 7: Census istius disciplinæ a Tiberio est.* ‘We may reckon Christianity from Tiberius.’ So ibid. c. 12: *inde censentur dei vestri. De Cor. c. 10: ex originis censu. Ad Natt. I. 12: omne omnino genus censum ad originem refert, quantoque genus censetur ordine, tanto origo convenitur in genere.* Hence census is ‘the class, rank, or order in which any person or thing is reckoned.’ Arnob. IV. p. 127: *Superorum retuleritis in censum.* Ad Natt. II. 1: *Triplici autem genere deorum censum distinxit. De Virg. <margin>CAP. VI.</margin> scilicet ob Dei offensam utrobique. Immo tu colis, qui facis, ut coli possint. Colis autem<sup>4</sup> non spiritu vilissimi nidoris alicujus, sed tui proprio, nec anima pecudis impensa, sed anima tua. Illis ingenium tuum immolas, illis sudorem tuum libas, illis prudentiam tuam accendis. Plus es illis quam sacerdos, cum per te habeant sacerdotem; diligentia tua<sup>5</sup> numen illorum est. Negas te quod facis colere? Sed illi non negant, quibus hanc saginationem et auratiorem et majorem<sup>6</sup> hostiam cædis, salutem tuam.

<margin>
CAP. VII.

How monstrous that Christian worshippers, nay, even Christian ministers, should be engaged in idol-making!
</margin>
Tota die ad hanc partem zelus fidei perorabit ingemens Christianum ab idolis in ecclesiam<sup>1</sup> venire, de adversaria officina in domum Dei venire, attollere

---

<sup>4</sup> *colis autem*. Observe the sacrificial terms *nidor*, *anima pecudis*, *immolas*, *libas*, *accendis*, *saginatus*, *auratus*, *major*, *hostiam cædere*.

<sup>5</sup> *diligentia tua*. Both Dodgson and Neander (Antignost. p. 228) translate *diligentia* as if it were nomin. It is no doubt abl. 'By your industry their divinity exists.'

<sup>6</sup> *auratiorem et majorem*. The horns of victims were gilded. The bull or cow was called *major hostia*, opposed to *minor hostia*, as the sheep, the goat, &c.

Ch. VII. <sup>1</sup> *ecclesiam*. See De Spect. c. 25, n. 3. Pamelius and La Cerda on this passage have collected thirty-three different names which the buildings set apart for divine service bore. Their object was to prove the existence of peculiar buildings for worship at a very early age. In the names which they bring forward, promiscuously, we observe various classes: 1 referring to the congregation, as *ecclesia*, *conventicula*; 2 to the sacred character of the place, as *domus Dei*, *dominicum*,

<column-break/>

*sacellum*; 3 to the purpose of meeting, as *oratorium*, <term>εὐκτήριον</term>, *proseucha*; 4 to the places where they met under persecution, as *martyrium*, *confessio*, *cæmeterium*, *cellæ*; 5 to buildings afterwards given them, as *basilica*, *templum*. The earlier names imply no more than stated places of meeting, which need not have been permanently secured for that purpose. Indeed, there could scarcely have been more so long as Christianity was at best tolerated. Another class of names refers us to the secret recesses of the catacombs, whither the Christians fled to celebrate in safety their common worship. The terms *basilicæ* and *templum* remind us how Constantine gave up *basilicæ* (judgment-halls) to the Christians, and how the Christian displaced heathen worship, and often occupied heathen sanctuaries. Tertul. contrasts the Christian place of worship with pagan splendour, Adv. Valent. c. 3: *Nostræ columbæ domus simplex in editis semper et apertis et ad lucem. Amat figura*

<columns>2</columns>
*Spiritus Sancti Orientem, Christi figuram.* Arnobius (vi. p. 189) would seem to deny the existence of peculiar buildings in his day. See above, c. 3, n. 1. But he is probably referring to buildings like heathen temples, as in p. 190, *templa illis non exstruimus*. In iv. p. 152, he mentions *conventicula, in quibus summus oratur Deus*. Bingham, Christ. ² *adorantur*, the hands of artificers are said to be worshipped in their handy-work. Rig. quotes De Resurr. Carn. c. 6: *Phidiæ manus Jovem Olympicum ex ebore molitæ adorantur*.

³ *eas manus admovere*. Comp. De Cor. c. 3. Bingh. Chr. Ant. xv. 5, 6, brings forward numerous passages to prove the antiquity of receiving the bread with the hands. Among them is the address of Ambrose to Theodosius, proving both this custom and that of giving the cup to the laity. The words are very apposite to these of Tertul.

<column-break/>

Theodoret. v. 18: Ποίοις ὀφθαλμοῖς ὄψει τοῦ κοινοῦ Δεσπότου νεών; ποίοις δὲ ποσὶν τὸ δάπεδον ἐκεῖνο πατήσεις τὸ ἅγιον; πῶς δὲ τὰς χεῖρας ἐκτενεῖς ἀποσταζούσας ἔτι τοῦ ἀδίκου φόνου τὸ αἷμα; πῶς δὲ τῷ στόματι προσοίσεις τὸ αἷμα τὸ τίμιον, τοσοῦτον διὰ τὸν τοῦ θυμοῦ λόγον ἐκχέας παρανόμως αἷμα; On Tertullian's doctrine of the Eucharist, in reference to the Romanist theory of transubstantiation, see Kaye’s Tertull. p. 426, foll.

⁴ *ordinem ecclesiasticum*. Special requirements as to morality were from the first exacted of such as should become ecclesiastics. See the Ordinances of the Church of Alexandria, in Bunsen’s Hippol. Vol. III. p. 31, foll.; Bingh. Chr. Ant. VI. 2, 1—6.

⁵ *viderit*. So Cod. Ag. and Gangn. Later editors altered it into *viderint*. ‘Let the Scripture, *If thy hand, &c.*, look to it, whether it be not something more than a mere figure of speech.’ On the use of *viderit*, see De Cor. c. 2.

<margin>CAP. VIII.</margin>
<margin>
To provide the furniture of idolatry is alike forbidden.
</margin>
Sunt et aliæ complurium artium species, quæ etsi non contingunt idolorum fabricationem, tamen ea, sine quibus idola nil possunt, eodem crimine expediunt. Nec enim differt, an exstruas vel exornes, si templum, si aram, si ædiculam ejus instruxeris, si bracteam expresseris¹, aut insignia aut etiam domum fabricaveris. Major est ejusmodi opera, quæ non effigiem confert, sed auctoritatem.

<margin>
Every trade may find innocent employment in supplying the wants of man.
</margin>
Si ita necessitas exhibitionis extenditur², habent³ et alias species, quæ sine exorbitatione disciplinæ, id est sine idoli confictura, opem victus præstent. Scit albarius tector⁴ et tecta sarcire et tectoria inducere et cisternam liare⁵ et cymatia⁶ distendere et multa alia ornamenta præter simulacra parietibus incrispare. Scit et pictor et marmorarius et ærarius et quicumque cælator latitudines suas⁷ utique multo faciliores. Nam qui signum describit, quanto facilius abacum linit! Qui de tilia Martem exsculpit, quanto citius armarium compingit! Nulla ars non alterius artis aut mater aut propinqua est. Nihil alterius vacat⁸. Tot sunt artium venæ, quot hominum concupiscen-

---

Ch. VIII. ¹ si bracteam expresseris, ‘If you shall have beaten out gold-leaf for the purpose of gilding images.’  The context shews that the purpose must be idolatrous, in order to make the act idolatrous.

<margin>CAP. VIII.</margin>
tiæ. Sed de mercedibus et manus pretiis interest. Proinde interest et de labore. Minor merces frequentiore actu repensatur. Quot parietes signa desiderant! Quot templa et ædes idolis ædificantur! Domus vero et insulæ⁹ quantæ! Soccus et baxa quotidie deaurantur, Mercurius et Serapis non quotidie. Sufficiat ad quæstum artificiorum: frequentior est¹⁰ omni superstitione luxuria et ambitio. Lances et scyphos facilius ambitio quam superstitio desiderabit. Coronas quoque magis luxuria, quam sollemnitas erogat. Cum igitur ad hæc artificiorum genera cohortemur, quæ idolum quidem et quæ idolo competunt non attingant, sint autem et hominibus communia sæpe quæ et idolis, hoc quoque cavere debemus, ne quid scientibus nobis ab aliquibus de manibus nostris in rem idolorum postuletur. Quod si concesserimus et non remediis tam usitatis egerimus¹¹, non puto nos a contagio idololatriæ vacare, quorum manus non ignorantium in officio vel in honore et usu dæmoniorum deprehenduntur.

Ch. IX. ¹ *astrologis*. The Coptic and Greek constitutions exclude magicians, enchanters, astrologers, diviners, users of magic verses, &c., from admission into the Church. Bunsen’s Hippol. Vol. III. p. 7; Bingh. Chr. Ant. XI. 5, 8.

² *istis diebus*, ‘in these days.’ Apul. Met. I. c. 4, *isto gemino obtutu*.

³ *immobili arbitrio*. This well-known pagan belief was entertained also by the Pharisees. Bull’s Harm. Apost. Diss. Post. XV. 18.

⁴ *amatores fœminarum*. This interpretation of Gen. vi. 2, was commonly, but not universally, received by early ecclesiastical writers. See in Oxf. Transl. a note on Apolog.

c. 22. Comp. De Orat. c. 22; De Virg. Vel. c. 7; De Spect. c. 4, n. 2.

Tacitus tells us of decrees de *mathematicis magisque Italia pellendis* under Tiberius, A.D. 16 (*Annal.* 2, 32), and under Claudius, A.D. 53 (*Annal.* 12, 52); but they were still privately allowed. In A.D. 70 we read, Hist. I. 22, *multos secreta Poppææ mathematicos......habuerant*. And again, Vitellius had to make an edict, A.D. 70, for their expulsion from Italy. Hist. II. 62. Comp. Juv. Sat. VI. 552, where the reliance on *Chaldæi* and *Mathematici* is exposed. *Magia* was forbidden in the XII. Tables, Apul. Apol. c. 47. Apuleius, in his

*Metam.* and *Apol.* shews the extent to which such arts were practised in his day. There is a detailed account of the system of Astrology, and the arts of Magic in the book of Hippolytus published as Origen's *Philosophumena* (Oxon. 1851,) Book IV.
6 *stellam*. Cod. Ag. and Gangn. *stellarum*. Gel. and Pamel. *stellas*, which is probably conjectural. Rig. from Ursinus, *stellam*, which seems a satisfactory emendation. Oehl. retains *stellarum*, explaining it, *stellarum Christi est (mathesis)*, and supposing an ellipse of *stellas* before *Saturni*, &c. Observe that Tertul. is speaking in strong irony: 'I suppose astrology is concerned about Christ, I suppose it is the star of Christ, not of Saturn, and of Mars,

<column-break/>

and of the other deified dead men that it watches and proclaims.' The argument is, 'If the magi of Scripture are to be an authority for astrology, astrologers ought to be occupied concerning Christ, and his star, as those magi were: whereas it is notorious that Saturn, Mars, and the like, are the stars observed by astrologers.'
7 *mortuorum*. Comp. *De Spect.* c. 6: *Proinde mortuis suis ut diis faciant*; below, c. 15: *Recogitemus omnem idololatriam in homines esse culturam, cum ipsos deos nationum homines retro fuisse etiam apud suos constet.*
8 *clausulam*, 'the close.' *Scorp.* c. 8, *Ipse (Johannes) clausula legis.* *Apolog.* c. 32, *clausulam sæculi*.

<margin>
CAP. X.
The occupation of a schoolmaster is idolatrous. A Christian master may not teach, though a Christian may learn in a heathen school.
</margin>
Quærendum autem est etiam de ludimagistris, sed et ceteris professoribus litterarum. Immo non dubitandum affines illos esse multimodæ idololatriæ. Primum quibus necesse est deos nationum prædicare, nomina, genealogias, fabulas, ornamenta honorifica quæque eorum enuntiare, tum sollemnia festaque eorumdem observare, ut quibus vectigalia sua supputent. Quis ludimagister sine tabula septem idolorum Quinquatria tamen frequentabit¹?

---

*tum qui gentibus orbem, Tempora quæ messor, quæ curvus arator haberet?* Id. *Æn.* vi. 850, *cælique meatus Describunt radio.* OEHL.

Ch. X. ¹ *Quis ludimagister...* Gelen. and Pamel. for *sine tabula septem*, read *si non tabularia*, which has been commonly adopted, but not satisfactorily explained. The reading of the text is that of Cod. Ag. and Gangn. Rigalt would prefer *non frequentabit?* but, as a less alteration, puts the interrogation after *idolorum?* and reads the rest as a separate sentence. ‘What schoolmaster is without his heathen calendar? or if he is, he will at all events keep the Quinquatria.’ The reading in the text seems more intelligible, ‘What schoolmaster will keep the

<column-break/>

Quinquatria (which he must of necessity keep) without a heathen calendar to reckon by?’ *Tabula septem idolorum*, ‘the calendar of the seven planets.’ Rig. quotes *Ad Natt.* i. c. 13: *Vos certe estis, qui etiam in laterculum septem dierum recepistis.* The *Ludi Quinquatrus* were held on the xiv. Kal. Apr. This was a general holiday-time for schoolboys. Hor. *Ep.* ii. 2, 197: *Ac potius, puer ut festis Quinquatribus olim, Exiguo gratoque fruaris tempore raptim.* Ovid, in behalf of the goddess Minerva, would propitiate certain stern pedagogues (who, I presume, were paid by the lesson) for their loss of payment during this festival by the hopes of new pupils and of the Minerval. *Fast.* iii. 829:

<margin>CAP. X.</margin> Ipsam primam novi discipuli stipem$^2$ Minervæ et honori et nomini consecrat, ut etsi non profanatus alicui idolo verbotenus de idolothyto esse dicatur, [pro idololatra vitetur]$^3$. Quid minus est inquinamenti eo quod$^4$ præstat quæstus et nominibus et honoribus idolo nuncupatus? Tam$^5$ Minervalia Minervæ, quam Saturnalia Saturni, quæ$^6$ etiam serviculis sub tempore Saturnalium celebrari necesse est. Etiam strenæ$^7$ captandæ et septimontium et brumæ et caræ cognationis honoraria$^8$ exigenda omnia, flore scholæ coronandæ; flaminicæ et ædiles sacrificant, creatis schola honoratur feriis. Idem fit idoli natali; omnis diaboli pompa frequentatur. Quis hæc competere Christiano existimabit, nisi

---

*Nec vos, turba, feri, censu fraudata, magistri,
Spernite, discipulos attrahet illa novos.*
*Frequentabit* does not imply actual attendance at the festival, but is equivalent to our word ‘celebrate.’

$^2$ *primam...stipem*. This payment was called *Minerval*. The master did not actually dedicate it, but receiving it in the name and in honour of Minerva, recognized it as sacred to a heathen goddess.

$^3$ *pro idololatra vitetur*. Cod. Ag. *pro idololatria vitatur*. Gel. and Pam. *pro idolatra vitetur*. Rig. omits the words, and I have enclosed them in a bracket. If we retain them, as Oehler does, we must translate thus, ‘so that although he be not said, as far as mere words go, to eat of what is offered to idols, having been bound over to the unholy service of any idol, he should be avoided as an idolater’... Omitting the words in the brackets, ‘so that although not bound over to the unholy service of any idol, as

<column-break/>

far as words go, he may be said to eat of what is offered to idols,’ i. e. in receiving support from an idolatrous payment. Dodgson, who follows Rigalt’s reading, connects *verbotenus* with *dicatur*.

$^4$ *eo quod*. Cod. Ag. and Gangn. *eo quem*. Gel. *quod*, omiss. *eo*. Pam. and Rig. *eo quod*. Oehl. by conjec. *eo, quam*, explaining *eo*, ‘scil. quod non profanatus alicui idolo verbotenus de idolothyto esse dicatur.’ The sentence is ironical: ‘What less pollution is there than that which is produced by a payment dedicated to an idol, both in name and honour.’

$^5$ *Tam*. Cod. Ag., Gangn. and Rig. *quam*. So also Oehl. who considers *quam...quam* to be used for *tam...quam*. See above, c. 4. But the instances there quoted differ from this, where the former sentence is the antecedent, and distinct from the relative sentence that follows: ‘The Minervalia are as much sacred to Minerva, as the Saturnalia to Saturn.’

<margin>CAP. X.</margin>
<margin>a.</margin>
qui putabit convenire etiam non magistro^9? Scimus dici posse, Si docere litteras dei servis non licet, etiam nec discere licebit, et, Quomodo quis instituetur ad prudentiam interim humanam, vel ad quemcumque sensum vel actum, cum instrumentum sit ad omnem vitam litteratura? Quomodo repudiamus sæcularia studia, sine quibus divina non possunt? Videamus igitur necessitatem litteratoriæ eruditionis, respiciamus ex parte eam admitti non posse, ex parte vitari. Fideles magis discere quam docere litteras capit^10; diversa est enim ratio discendi et docendi. Si fidelis litteras doceat, insertas idolorum prædicationes sine dubio, dum

---

^6 *quæ*. Cod. Ag. and Gangn. *quem etiam serviculi*. Gel., Pamel. and Rig. *quæ etiam serviculis*. Oehl. retains *quem* on account of *sub tempore Saturnalium* in the same sentence. But the antecedent of *quæ* is not ‘the festival of the Saturnalia,’ as in *sub tempore Saturnalium*, but, ‘the presents exchanged at the time of the Saturnalia.’ Lipsius (*Saturnal.* I. c. 16) quotes this passage, illustrating it from Sueton. *Aug. c. 75: Saturnalibus... munera dividebat*; and from Spartianus, *Hadr.: Saturnalicia et Sigillaricia frequenter amicis inopinantibus misit, et ipse ab his libenter accepit.*

^7 *strenæ*, (gifts presented on any religious day), especially New Year’s day, connected with the worship of Janus. Ov. Fast. I. 183, foll. These gifts were received even by the emperor from the people, and applied by him to certain public works. Ursinus in Sueton. Aug. c. 91, quotes inscriptions found in the Via Sacra commemorating the erection of certain buildings, *ex stipe, quam Pop. Rom. anno novo abeunti contulit.*

<column-break/>

Septimontium, a gift presented to magistrates on III. Id. Decemb., on which day the enclosure of the city was celebrated. Tertull. (*Ad Natt.* II. c. 15) speaks of a god Septimontius. *Brumæ*, gifts of the winter solstice, like our Christmas presents.

^8 *caræ cognationis honoraria*, presents given at the Caristia, a festival celebrated XI. Kal. Mart., consecrated to the memory of departed relatives. Ovid. Fast. II. 617 foll.

^9 *etiam non magistro*. ‘If a Christian who is not a schoolmaster may fitly join in these idolatrous rites, then, and then only, may a schoolmaster do so.’ A schoolmaster’s business makes these rites necessary for him. A Christian to whom they were not necessary would not think of practising them. The necessity shews the business to be unlawful.

^10 *capit. Gr.* ἐνδέχεται. Oehl. *De Fuga*. c. 1, quotes *Apolog.* c. 17, *æstimari non capit. De Pudic.* c. *capit et indulgeri. De Bapt.* c. 1, *capit numerari*, and other passages.
