CAP. XI.
Commerce is at best doubtful, considering its origin; but that commerce is surely forbidden (the commerce in incense and the like) which respects the very essence of idolatry. 1 Tim. vi. 10. 1 Tim. i. 19. Col. iii. 5. Matt. v. 33, 34.
</margin>
De generationibus si¹ cetera delictorum recogitemus, imprimis cupiditatem radicem omnium malorum qua quidam irretiti circa fidem naufragium sunt passi² (quamvis³ et idololatria ab eodem apostolo dicta sit cupiditas), tum mendacium cupiditatis ministrum (taceo de perjurio, quando ne jurare quidem⁴ liceat)—negotiatio servo Dei apta est? Ceterum si cupiditas abscedat, quæ est causa acquirendi? Cessante causa acquirendi non erit necessitas negotiandi. Sit nunc⁵ aliqua justitia quæstus, secura de cupiditatis et mendacii observatione, in crimen offendere idololatriæ eam opinor, quæ ad ipsam idolorum animam et spiritum⁶ pertinet, quæ omne dæmonium saginat. Sane; non illa principalis idololatriæ? Viderint, si⁷ eædem merces, thura dico

---

causes [of commerce] we review the other vices; first, covetousness, the root of all evils, in which some having been ensnared, have made shipwreck of their faith (although covetousness has by the same apostle been also called idolatry): then falsehood, the handmaid of covetousness (not to speak of perjury, seeing we are forbidden even to swear)—[when we see, I say, that commerce has its origin in covetousness, and in falsehood, we are tempted to ask in general] Is commerce fitted for the servants of God?' Comp. *Ecclus.* xxvi. 29.

² *naufragium sunt passi*. The phrase occurs in 1 Tim. i. 19. In 1 Tim. vi. 10, we find ἧς τινὲς ὀρεγόμενοι ἀπεπλανήθησαν ἀπὸ τῆς πίστεως. We may observe that Tertull. quotes from the Greek, and not

<column-break/>

from the Vulgate, both in the LXX. and in the N. T.; an incidental proof of the antiquity of his works.

³ *quamvis*... Although covetousness has been also called idolatry, and therefore can scarcely be considered a vice distinct from that of idolatry.

⁴ *ne jurare quidem*. See below, c. 17 and c. 23.

⁵ *Sit nunc*, on the use of subj. see *De Spect.* c. 6, n. 3.

⁶ *idolorum animam et spiritum*. Comp. Arnob. vii. p. 233: *Ergo si et dii spirant, odoremque ad se attrahunt auris comitantibus involutum, non est ab re dicere et alienis suffitionibus vivere et interclusis posse spiraminibus vivere.*

⁷ *viderint, si*. 'Let the wares look to it, and see how they can defend themselves.' 'No matter, if.' On *viderint*, see *De Cor.* c. 2, n. 3.

<margin>CAP. XI.</margin> et cetera peregrinitatis sacrificium idolorum etiam hominibus ad pigmenta medicinalia^8, nobis quoque^9 insuper ad solatia sepulturæ usui sunt. Certe cum pompæ, cum sacerdotia, cum sacrificia idolorum de periculis, de damnis, de incommodis, de cogitationibus, de discursibus negotiationibusve instruuntur, quid aliud quam procurator idolorum demonstraris? Nemo contendat posse hoc modo omnibus negotiationibus controversiam fieri. Graviora delicta quæque pro magnitudine periculi diligentiam extendunt observationis, ut non ab his tantum abscedamus, sed et ab iis per quæ fiunt^10. Licet enim ab aliis fiat, non interest, si per me. In nullo necessarius esse debeo alii, cum facit quod mihi non licet. Ex hoc, quod vetor facere, intelligere debeo curandum mihi esse, ne fiat per me. Denique^11 in alia causa non levioris reatus præjudicium istud observo. Nam quod mihi de stupro interdictum sit, aliis ad eam

CAP. XI. dæmonia thurarius? Nam facilius sine idolo idololatria, quam sine thurarii merce. Ipsius fidei conscientiam perrogemus. Quo ore Christianus thurarius, si per templa transibit, quo ore fumantes aras despuet et exsufflabit$^{18}$, quibus ipse prospexit? Qua constantia exorcizabit alumnos suos$^{19}$, quibus domum suam cellarium præstat? Ille quidem si excluserit dæmonium, non sibi placeat de fide$^{20}$. Neque enim inimicum exclusit. Facile debuit de eo impetrare, quem quotidie pascit. Nulla igitur ars, nulla professio, nulla negotiatio, quæ quid aut instruendis aut formandis idolis administrat, carere poterit titulo idololatriæ; nisi si aliud omnino interpretemur idololatriam, quam famulatum idolorum colendorum.

<margin>
As to gaining a livelihood, we must remember that we are to sacrifice profit, ease, and plenty, for Christ's sake.

CAP. XII. fidei. Nemo eorum, quos Dominus allegit, Non habeo, dixit, quo vivam. Fides tamen non timet. Scit etiam famem non minus sibi contemnendam propter Deum, quam omne mortis genus. Didicit non respicere vitam, quanto magis victum? Quotusquisque hæc adimplevit? Sed quæ penes homines difficilia, penes Deum facilia.

<margin>C. XIII. We must give up all that savours of heathen superstition, sacrifices, shows and festivals:</margin>
*Sic tamen<sup>1</sup> nobis de mansuetudine et clementia Dei blandiamur, ut non usque ad idololatriæ affinitates necessitatibus largiamur, sed omnem afflatum ejus vice pestis etiam de longinquo devitemus, non in his tantum quæ præmisimus, sed in universa serie humanæ superstitionis, sive deis suis, sive defunctis, sive regibus mancipatæ, ut ad eosdem spiritus immundos pertinentis, modo per sacrificia et sacerdotia, modo per spectacula et hoc genus, modo per festos dies.

<margin>We must abstain from festivals, for we may not rejoice with the world, nor be ashamed of the name of Christ.</margin>

Ch. XIII. <sup>1</sup> *Sic tamen.* It is usual to begin a fresh paragraph here. But Oehler, (in whose edition *si tamen* is read apparently by a misprint,) remarks that this sentence should be connected with what precedes. The argument is this: It is true that few fulfil this requirement. But God is so merciful, that what is impossible with men is possible with God, and so even those who do not fulfil it may enter into His kingdom. However, we must not so presume

<margin>C. XIII.</margin> quid participas idolothyta sollemnia, quasi tibi quoque præscriptum sit de die, quominus id, quod homini debes vel tibi ab homine debetur, citra diei observationem luas vel recipias. Da formam^8, in quam velis agi tecum. Cur enim et lateas, cum <margin>1 Cor. viii. 7.</margin> ignorantia alterius tuam conscientiam contamines? Si non ignoraris quod sis Christianus, tentaris et contra conscientiam alterius agis tanquam non Christianus. Enimvero et dissimulaberis. Tentatus, addictus es^9. Certe sive hac sive illac, reus es confusionis in Deo. Qui autem confusus super <margin>Luc. ix. 26.</margin> me fuerit penes homines, et ego confundar super illo, inquit, penes Patrem meum, qui est in cælis.

<margin>
C. XIV.
We may not propitiate our enemies by joining in unlawful festivals, much less join in such festivals unsolicited.
</margin> Sed enim plerique jam induxerunt animo agnoscendum^1 esse, si quando, quæ ethnici, faciunt, ne nomen^2 blasphemetur. Porro blasphemia, quæ nobis omnino devitanda est, hæc, opinor, est, si quis nostrum ad justam blasphemiam ethnicum de-

---

<columns>2</columns>
^8 *Da formam...* The dilemma is this: In observing these days, you are either not known, or you are known to be a Christian. If you are not known, you defile your own conscience in disguising your profession by such compliances; if you are known to be a Christian, you wound the conscience of others (giving them a false idea of what Christianity is) by doing what a Christian ought not to do.

^9 *Enimvero et dissimulaberis. Tentatus, addictus es.* Thus in Cod. Ag., Gangn. and Gel. The passage has been variously amended. Pamel.: *Enimvero etsi simulaveris.* Latinius and Junius: *sin vero et dissimulaveris.* Rig.: *Enimvero et dissimulaveris.* Oehl.: *sin vero et*

<column-break/>

*dissimulaberis.* The agreement of the early MSS. of different families in a reading, whose meaning is at least not obvious, makes for, not against its genuineness. I have therefore retained the ancient reading, with a punctuation slightly different from that usually adopted. The first clause is connected with what precedes. 'You act as if you were no Christian. For in truth, even though known to be one, it will be pretended that you are not a Christian.' The heathen, though they know you to be a Christian, will wink at it, and will be glad to accept the act as a virtual abnegation of your faith. Tertullian then goes on to state the consequence which follows on either alternative: 'You have

Sabbata, inquit, vestra et neomenias et ceremonias odit anima mea. Nobis, quibus sabbata extranea sunt^8 et neomeniæ et feriæ a Deo aliquando dilectæ, Saturnalia et Januariæ et Brumæ^9 et Matronales^10 frequentantur, munera commeant, strenæ consonant^11, lusus, convivia constrepunt. O melior fides nationum in suam sectam, quæ nullam sollemnitatem Christianorum sibi vindicat ! Non Dominicum diem, non Pentecosten, etiamsi nossent, nobiscum communicassent : timerent enim, ne Christiani viderentur. Nos, ne ethnici pronuntiemur, non veremur. Si quid et carni indulgendum est, habes, non dicam tuos dies tantum, sed et plures. Nam ethnicis semel annuus dies quisque festus est, tibi octavo quoque die. Excerpe singulas sollemnitates nationum et in ordinem exsere, Pentecosten^12 implere non poterunt.

Recogitemus omnem idololatriam in homines esse culturam, cum ipsos deos nationum homines retro fuisse$^2$ etiam apud suos constet. <margin>C. XVI.</margin>
<margin>In festivals of marriage, and the like, which originate not in idolatry, it is lawful to take part, provided we join in no idolatrous act that may accompany the celebration.</margin>

Circa officia vero privatarum et communium sollemnitatum, ut togæ puræ¹, ut sponsalium, ut nuptialium, ut nominalium², nullum putem periculum observari de flatu idololatriæ, quæ intervenit. Causæ enim sunt considerandæ, quibus præstatur officium. Eas mundas esse opinor per semetipsas, quia neque vestitus virilis neque annulus aut conjunctio maritalis de alicujus idoli honore descendit. <margin>C. XVII. Christian slaves may serve heathen</margin>
Ceterum quid facient servi vel liberi^1 fideles,

---

variations from Gangn. look very much like emendations, to make out the sense. Others have corrected it conjecturally: as Ursinus and La Cerda, *Si sim vocatus* ; Latinius and Junius, *Si nec in vocatu*. Rigalt, looking as usual to the Cod. Ag., adopts its reading, only altering *ad sacrificiis* to *adsacrificii*, which word he illustrates by *admilitia*, from the Pandects. The explanation which Rigalt offers is this: Tertullian is contemplating the case of slaves called upon to assist in such ceremonies. 'Suppose I am called upon to take part, and the performance of my duty and service does not bear the title of assisting in sacrifice; let the heathen master do as much as he pleases, would only it were as much as he pleased himself to do, and we might avoid seeing, &c.' Oehler adopts Rigalt's reading, and expresses his general concurrence in Rigalt's explanation, illustrating the use of *adsacrificii* by *adtradux*, *De Pat.* c. 5, and other instances. So Dodgson translates it. I differ from them, because, 1 the case of slaves seems to be first introduced in c. 17, and the subject here is the general question

<column-break/>

of attendance on such ceremonies ; 2 the words *vocatus* and *officium* rather imply invitation than command ; 3 the ellipse supposed before *quantum sibi libet* is very harsh. Oehl. endeavours to lessen the harshness, by placing a comma before *et operæ*, and interpreting the sentence, 'Suppose that I have been summoned, &c., let even the performance of my service be at its pleasure' (*etiam opera mea sibi expungatur in quantum sibi libet*). As to the reading of the text, I observe, 1 that *Si invocatus* may very probably have been a mistake made by Gangn. in reading his MSS., expecting to find *si*; 2 that in Cod. Ag. *ad sacrificiis* is clearly erroneous, and that the MS. is more likely to be faulty where it disagrees than where it agrees with others ; 3 *quæ tum* in Gangn. is plainly wrong, and *quantum* right ; 4 that *quantum sibi* in Cod. Ag. in the next sentence is very probably the repetition of a copyist from the former sentence. But if *quantum sibi* be retained, it may be explained, as will be shewn. For the explanation:—All commentators have supposed the answer to *sim vocatus* to lie in *quan-*

<margin>C. XVII.</margin>
<margin>masters even when sacrificing, provided they themselves abstain from any idolatrous act.</margin>
item officiales sacrificantibus dominis vel patronis vel præsidibus suis adhærentes? Sed si merum quis sacrificanti tradiderit, immo si verbo quoque aliquo sacrificio necessario adjuverit, minister habebitur idololatriæ. Hujus regulæ memores etiam magistratibus et potestatibus officium possumus reddere secundum patriarchas et ceteros majores, qui regibus idololatris usque ad finem idololatriæ apparuerunt².

---

*tum sibi libet*. I believe the hypothetic case to include from *sim* to *libet*; and the answer to be in *licebit*. But the direct answer is interrupted by the wish *Utinam quidem*; the *quidem* marking as it so often does that the sentence is introduced by way of contrast, the main sentence being that which begins with *sed*, as in Greek μὲν...δὲ. Tertull. has been saying that ceremonies of a particular kind may be attended. But an objection is started, 'Sacrifices are often mixed with them.' Tertull. puts a case: 'Suppose that I have been invited, and suppose both that the name of my attendance is not connected with sacrifices, and that the performance of my part is at pleasure.' *Sibi* referring to *opera mea*: *quantum sibi libet* is illustrated by Oehl. from Adv. Hermog. c. 23: *Fuerit licet materia quantum sibi licet vel potius Hermogeni*, and answers very much to the phrase *ad libitum*. Tertull. proceeds to answer the hypothetical case: 'Although I could wish we were exempt even from seeing idolatrous sacrifices; yet, situated as we are, it will be lawful to be present, &c.' He then proceeds with no doubt to the case where a person is invited to a sacrifice: *Plane ad sacerdotium, etc.* If

<column-break/>

we read with Rig. *Utinam quantum sibi quidem, nec*, we must interpret, 'Would that it were indeed at its own pleasure,' i. e. 'Would that the performance of the Christian's part were really so free, as that he were excused from seeing what he would not do.' For the use of the subj. *sim*, see De Spect. c. 6, n. 3. *Officium* is especially applied to complimentary attendance upon nuptial feasts. Sueton. Calig. c. 25: *Liviam...nubentem, cum ad officium et ipse venisset, ad se deduci imperavit.* Claud. c. 26: *confecit nuptias, non repertis qui sequerentur exemplum, excepto libertino quodam et altero primipilari, cujus officium nuptiarum et ipse cum Agrippina celebravit.*
<sup>5</sup> *circumdedit sæculum idololatria*. Comp. De Spect. c. 8. *Malus*, sc. *Satanas*. Below, c. 21.
Ch. XVII. <sup>1</sup> *liberi*. La Cerda, Rig. and Oehl. *liberti*. La C. remarks that *servi* corresponds to *dominis*, *liberti* to *patronis*, *officiales* to *præsidibus*. But Dionys. Hal. (II. 22) tells us that priests were assisted by their children, if they had any, and if not, by children of other families called *camilli*. Probably *liberi* refers to these.
<sup>2</sup> *apparuerunt*. See De Spect. c. 17, n. 8.

<margin>
**C. XVII.**
The question whether Christians may bear office in heathen countries must be answered in the negative, because the insignia of magistrates have their origin in idolatry.
**Gen. xli. 42.**
**Dan. v. 29.**
</margin>

Hinc proxime disputatio oborta est, an servus Dei alicujus dignitatis aut potestatis administrationem capiat, si ab omni specie idololatriæ intactum se aut gratia aliqua aut astutia etiam præstare possit, secundum quod et Joseph et Daniel mundi ab idololatria et dignitatem et potestatem administraverunt in ornamento et purpura præfecturæ totius Ægypti sive Babyloniæ. Cedamus itaque succedere alicui posse, ut in quoquo honore in solo honoris nomine incedat, neque sacrificet, neque sacrificiis auctoritatem suam accommodet, non hostias locet³, non curas templorum deleget, non vectigalia eorum procuret, non spectacula edat de suo aut de publico aut edendis præsit, nihil sollemne pronuntiet vel edicat, ne juret quidem; jam vero quæ sunt potestatis, neque judicet de capite alicujus aut pudore⁴ (feras enim de pecunia), neque damnet neque prædamnet⁵, neminem vinciat, neminem recludat aut torqueat, si hæc credibile

---

³ **hostias locet.** *locare*, ‘to put out the contract;’ *redimere*, ‘to take it up.’ Above, c. 11: *publicarum victimarum redemptor.* Plaut. Capt. IV. 2, 39: *Qui locant cædendos agnos.* Aulul. III. 6, 32: *loces efferendum.* Hor. Sat. I. 8, 9: *ejecta cadavera cellis Conservus vili portanda locabat in arca*, where some interpret *locabat* simply ‘placed.’

⁴ **pudore**, ‘honor.’ Neander (Antignost. p. 232), illustrates this by Apolog. c. 4: *In pudoris notam capitis pœna conversa.* Comp. De Cor. c. 11: *Et vincula et carcerem et tormenta et supplicia administrabit, nec suarum ultor injuriarum?*

⁵ **neque damnet...** ‘Damnat ju-

<column-break/>

dex, prædamnat legislàtor. Utroque abstineat Christianus, neque de capite cujusquam aut legem aut sententiam ferat.’ **RIG.**

**Ch. XVIII.** ¹ **Jam vero.** The separation of this sentence from the foregoing, in order to begin a new Chapter with a new paragraph, has misled interpreters. The actual answer to the question as to magistracy is only here commenced. ‘Suppose,’ says Tertul., ‘for argument’s sake, that a Christian magistrate could avoid all those duties, ordinary and extraordinary, which are the especial functions of magistrates; yet an examination of the mere insignia of office will shew them to be

<margin>c. XVIII.</margin> Belem nec draconem colere, quod multo postea apparuit. Simplex igitur purpura illa nec jam dignitatis erat, sed ingenuitatis apud barbaros insigne. Quodammodo^6 enim et Joseph, qui servus fuerat, et Daniel, qui per captivitatem statum verterat, civitatem Babyloniam et Ægyptum sunt consecuti per habitum barbaricæ ingenuitatis. Sic penes nos quoque fideles, si necesse fuerit, poterit et pueris prætexta concedi, et puerilis stola, nativitatis insignia, non potestatis, generis, non honoris, ordinis, non superstitionis. Ceterum purpura vel cetera insignia dignitatum et potestatum insertæ dignitati et potestatibus idololatriæ ab initio dicata profanationis suæ maculam, <margin><unclear>has</unclear></margin> cum præterea ipsis etiam idolis induantur prætextæ et trabeæ et laticlavi, fasces quoque et virgæ præferantur, et merito. <margin>Joh. xii. 31. Eph. vi. 12.</margin> Nam dæmonia magistratus sunt sæculi hujus; unius collegii insignia fasces et purpuras gestant. Quid ergo proficies, si suggestu quidem utaris, opera ejus vero

<margin>c. XVIII.</margin> gestu, quam dignitatis quam <gloss>11</gloss> potestatis. Quis enim magis his usus fuisset, quam Dei filius? Quales et quanti eum fasces producerent! qualis purpura de humeris ejus floreret! quale aurum de capite radiaret, nisi gloriam sæculi alienam et sibi et suis judicasset! Igitur quæ noluit, rejecit; quæ rejecit, damnavit; quæ damnavit, in pompa diaboli deputavit. Non enim damnasset, nisi non sua: alterius autem esse non possent, nisi diaboli, quæ Dei non sunt. Tu si diaboli pompam ejerasti, quicquid ex ea attigeris, id scias esse idololatriam. Vel hoc te commonefaciat omnes hujus sæculi potestates et dignitates non solum alienas, verum et inimicas Dei esse, quod per illas adversus Dei servos supplicia consulta sunt, per illas et pœnæ ad impios paratæ ignorantur <gloss>12</gloss>. Sed et nativitas et substantia tua molestæ tibi sunt adversus idololatriam. Ad evitandum <gloss>13</gloss> remedia deesse non possunt, cum et, si defuerint, supersit unicum illud <gloss>14</gloss>, quo felicior factus non in terris magistratus sed in cælis.

---

ab omni *gloriæ et dignitatis ardore frigentibus*. The agreement of MSS. seems to establish *dirigendo*. There can be little doubt that this is the gerund of *dirigo*, in the sense of ‘measure,’ ‘adapt to a rule,’ ‘bring to a level,’ as Cic. De Orat. III. 49 : *Nec sunt hæc rhythmicorum et musicorum acerrima norma dirigenda*. ‘He gave to his own a pattern to level all the high-flown consequence of greatness and power.’
<gloss>11</gloss> *quam...quam*. See above, c. 4, n. 7.
<gloss>12</gloss> *ignorantur*. So Cod. Ag., Gangn., Gel. and Pamel. ...*irrogantur*, Latin., Junius and Rig., who,

<column-break/>

however, in his note approves and explains *ignorantur*, ‘It is owing to the powers of the world that punishments are devised against the Christians; it is owing to them that the penalties decreed by God against the impious are unknown (because their persecutions prevent the spread of Christianity, which reveals these penalties).’
<gloss>13</gloss> The remainder of this treatise is wanting in the Cod. Ag.
<gloss>14</gloss> *unicum illud*, i. e. martyrdom.
Ch. XIX. <gloss>1</gloss> *caligata*. Caliga, the boot worn by common soldiers (*gregarii*). Hence *caligati* were opposed to the officers. Tac. Ann. I.

<margin>
C. XIX.
A soldier's occupation is unchristian.
</margin>

Possit in isto capitulo etiam de militia definitum videri, quæ inter dignitatem et potestatem est. At nunc de isto quæritur, an fidelis ad militiam converti possit, et an militia ad fidem admitti etiam caligata¹ vel inferior quæque, cui non sit necessitas immolationum vel capitalium judiciorum. Non convenit sacramento divino et humano, signo Christi² et signo diaboli, castris lucis et castris tenebrarum; non potest una anima duobus deberi, Deo et Cæsari.

Exod. xxviii. 12.
Matt. iii. 4.
Exod. xxxi. 23.

CAP. XX.
We must not from habit call the heathen deities gods, or give them divine honours.

69: *nihil relictum imperatoribus, ubi femina manipulos intervisat, signa adeat, largitionem tentet, tanquam parum ambitiose filium ducis gregali habitu circumferat Cæsaremque Caligulam appellari velit.* See *De Cor.* c. 1, n. 8.

### CAP. XX. non factis tantum, verum etiam verbis periclitetur (nam sicut scriptum est, Ecce homo et facta ejus², <margin>Matt. xii. 37.</margin> ita, Ex ore tuo justificaberis), meminisse debemus etiam in verbis quoque idololatriæ incursum præcavendum aut de consuetudinis vitio aut timiditatis. <margin>Exod. xxiii. 13.</margin> Deos nationum nominari lex prohibet, non utique ne nomina eorum pronuntiemus, quæ nobis ut diximus conversatio extorquet. Nam id plerumque dicendum est, In templo Æsculapii illum habes, et, In vico Isidis habito, et, Sacerdos Jovis factus est, et multa alia in hunc modum, quando et hominibus hoc genus nomina³ inducuntur. Neque enim Saturnum honoro, si ita vocavero eum suo nomine; tam non honoro, quam Marcum, si vocavero Marcum. <margin>Exod. xxiii. 13.</margin> Sed ait, Nomen aliorum deorum ne commemoremini⁴, neque audiatur de tuo ore. Hoc præcepit, ne deos vocemus illos. Nam et in prima parte legis, <margin>Exod. xx. 7.</margin> Non sumes, inquit, nomen Domini Dei tui in vano, id est idolo⁵. Cecidit igitur in idololatriam qui idolum nomine Dei honoraverit. Quod si deos dicendum erit⁶, adjiciendum est aliquid quo appareat, quia non ego illos deos dico. Nam et Scriptura deos nominat, sed adjicit suos vel nationum;

---

² **Ecce homo et facta ejus.** This is also quoted by S. August. Medit. c. 39. Some suppose this to have been taken from the book of Enoch. Bp Kaye (Tertull. p. 300) gives other instances of Tertullian's misquotations of Scripture, which are probably to be ascribed to forgetfulness. In this chapter in quoting from the 96th Psalm, he substitutes *dæmonia* for *idola*. See below, c. 23, n. 9.

³ *hoc genus nomina.* See above, c. 11, n. 12.

<column-break/>

⁴ *commemoremini.* Oehl. illustrates the use of the deponent verb from S. Aug. Enar. in Ps. liv. : *Homines malos, quos patitur, commemoratus est.*

⁵ *in vano, id est, idolo.* Pamel. tells us that Jerome interprets 1 Sam. xii. 21, *nolite declinare post vana*, by *post idola*, and refers to Acts xiv. 15, *ἀπὸ τούτων τῶν ματαίων*, to *idols*. See above, c. 10, n. 12.

⁶ *deos dicendum erit*, a Grecism. Lucret. 1. 139 : *Multa novis verbis*

<margin>CAP. XX.</margin>
<margin>Ps. xcvi. 1.</margin>
sicut David, cum deos nominasset, ubi ait, Dei autem nationum dæmonia. Sed hoc mihi ad sequentia magis præstructum est. Ceterum consuetudinis vitium est Mehercule dicere, Medius Fidius, accedente ignorantia quorundam, qui ignorant jusjurandum esse per Herculem. Porro quid erit dejeratio per eos quos ejerasti, quam^7 prævaricatio fidei cum idololatria? quis enim, per quos dejerat, non honorat?

<margin>C. XXI.</margin>
<margin>We must not be led astray by the words of others, either acquiescing when they swear by false gods, or shewing anger when they curse us by such names;</margin>
Timiditatis est autem, cum te alius per deos suos obligat juratione vel aliqua testificatione, et tu, ne intelligaris^1, quiescis. Nam æque quiescendo confirmas majestatem eorum, cujus causa videberis obligatus. Quid refert, deos nationum dicendo deos an audiendo confirmes? jures per idola, an ab alio adjuratus acquiescas? Cur non agnoscamus versutias Satanæ, qui, quod ore nostro perficere non potest, id agit, ut suorum ore perficiat per aures inferens nobis idololatriam? Certe quisquis ille est, aut amica aut inimica congressione astringit. Si inimica, jam ad pugnam vocaris, et scis tibi dimicandum esse: si amica, quanto securius in Dominum transferes sponsionem^2 tuam, ut dissolvas

---

*præsertim quum sit agendum. Ibid.* II. 491: *Addendum partes alias erit.* Oehl. quotes De Pall. c. 4: *Physconem et Sardanapalum tacendum erit.*

^7 *quid erit...quam.* See De Cor. c. 14, D. 3.

Ch. XXI. ^1 *ne intelligaris*, 'lest you be understood to be a Christian.' Comp. above, c. 13.

^2 *sponsionem.* So Gangn. Pamel., Gel. and Rig. *responsionem.* *Sponsio*, on the part of him who binds

<column-break/>

himself, is opposed to *obligatio*, on the part of him who binds him. The party who would bind you to a certain covenant, in the name of a heathen god, meets you either as an enemy, or as a friend; 'if as an enemy, you are called to the battle, and must fight: if as a friend, with how little risk will you transfer your acceptance of the covenant to the name of the Lord, and so do away with the form of binding used by

<margin>c. XXI.</margin> obligationem ejus, per quem te Malus honori idolorum, id est, idololatriæ quærebat annectere? Omnis patientia ejusmodi idololatria. Honoras eos, quibus impositis obsequium præstitisti. Scio quendam, cui Dominus ignoscat, cum illi in publico per litem dictum esset, Jupiter tibi sit iratus, respondisse, Immo tibi$^3$. Quid aliter fecisset ethnicus, qui Jovem deum credidit? Etiamsi non per eundem retorsisset maledictum, nec per ullum Jovis similem, confirmaverat Jovem deum, per quem se maledictum indigne tulisse demonstraverat remaledicens. Ad quid enim indigneris per eum, quem scis nihil esse? Nam si insanis$^4$, jam esse confirmas, et erit idololatriæ$^5$ professio timoris tui: quanto magis, cum per ipsum remaledicis, eodem Jovis honorem$^6$ facis, quo et ille qui te provocavit?

<margin>Matt. v. 44. 1 Pet. iii. 9.</margin> Fidelis autem in ejusmodi$^7$ ridere debet, non insanire, immo secundum præceptum, ne per Deum quidem remaledicere, sed plane benedicere per Deum, ut et idola destruas et Deum prædices et adimpleas disciplinam.

---

<margin>C. XXII. or allowing them to bless us in these same names, and so tacitly acknowledging false divinities.</margin> Æque benedici per deos nationum Christo ini-

---

<columns>2</columns>

him, through whose instrumentality Satan sought to bind you to the service of idols, *i. e.* to idolatry.'

$^3$ *Immo tibi*. La Cerda quotes Mart. *Ep. v. 63: Hoc sentis? inquis: faciat tibi sic bene Cæsar, Sic Capitolinus Jupiter—immo tibi*.

$^4$ *insanis*, 'you are furious,' as just below, *ridere debes, non insanire*.

$^5$ *idolatriae*. Gangn., Gel. and Pamel. as in text. Junius, Latin. and Rig. *idololatria*. La Cerda, *timor tuus*. No change is necessary. 'The profession of your fear will be the profession of idolatry.'

<column-break/>

$^6$ *Jovis honorem*. Here again Rig. deviates from the old reading, substituting *Jovi* for *Jovis*. Oehl. retains *Jovis*, comparing above in this chapter *honori idolorum*.

### C. XXIII.

verbo bis acuta et infesta utrinque¹, licet tibi blandiatur quasi vacet in utroque, dum factum non videtur, quia dictum non tenetur². Pecuniam de ethnicis mutuantes sub pignoribus fiduciati³ jurati cavent⁴ et negant se scire. Volunt⁵ scilicet tempus persecutionis, et locus tribunalis, et persona præ<margin>Matt. v. 34.</margin>sidis⁶. Præscribit Christus non esse jurandum. Scripsi, inquit, sed nihil dixi. Lingua, non littera occidit. Hic ego naturam et conscientiam⁷ advoco: naturam, quia nihil potest manus scribere, etiamsi lingua in dictando cesset immobilis et quieta, quod non anima dictaverit; quanquam et ipsi linguæ anima dictaverit aut a se conceptum aut ab alio

---

writing is essentially the same as speaking, and therefore it is a sin in word; 2 and further, that to write an oath, is to swear an oath, and therefore it is a sin in act, just as swearing by false gods is an act of apostasy.

¹ **bis infesta et acuta utrimque**. It is ‘two-edged,’ because it is idolatry both in word and in deed, as will be shewn.

² **dictum non tenetur**. Not ‘because the word is not kept,’ but ‘nothing is thought to have been done, because nothing is held to have been spoken.’

³ **fiduciati**. So Gangn., Gel. and Pam. Rig. by conj. *fiduciatis*. *fiduciati*, ‘entrusted with money upon security.’

⁴ **jurati cavent**. Comp. Sueton. Calig. c. 12: *pollicitus et matrimonium suum, si imperio potitus fuisset, deque ea re jurejurando et syngrapha cavit.*

⁵ **et negant se scire. Volunt.** Gangn. *et se necant, se scire volunt.*

<column-break/>

Gel. and Pam. *et sic negant. Scire volunt.* Jun. and Rig. *et sic negant scire. Volunt.* Oehl. *et se negant; se scire volunt.* Oehler's explanation is unsatisfactory. The reading of the text involves a very slight deviation from the ancient reading.

traditum. Jam ne dicatur, Alius dictavit; conscientiam appello, an quod alius dictavit anima suscipiat et sive comitante sive residente lingua ad manum transmittat. Cavisti igitur, quod in cor tuum plane ascendit, quod neque ignorasse te contendere potes neque noluisse. Nam cum caveres, scisti, cum scires, utique voluisti; et es tam in facto^11 quam cogitatu, nec potes leviore crimine majus excludere, ut dicas falsum plane effici, cavendo quod non facis^12. ---

shame the Christian by shewing that the threat of persecution enjoins this compliance, while Christ condemns it.

^7 *naturam et conscientiam.* The plea is, That is no oath which is written, not spoken. Tertull. in answer, 1 appeals to the nature of the thing, shewing that the soul must dictate what the hand writes; and that if you say, the soul may dictate either what is conceived by itself, or what is delivered by another, there is precisely the same ambiguity in the case of speaking: 'to the tongue also the soul may have dictated what has been conceived by itself, or what has been delivered by another.' So there is no foundation for any difference between the *lingua* and *littera*, on that ground; 2 he appeals to the conscience whether, in the case of any dictation, the soul does not first receive, and, whether the tongue be aiding or be inactive, transmit the words to the hand. But the Lord has declared us to be liable for the act, if the thought has come into

<column-break/>

the heart. Your bond then must have come into your heart. Therefore you cannot plead ignorance, or absence of intention.

^8 *bene quod.* See above, c. 5, n. 10.

^9 *in cor hominis ascenderit.* Tertullian quotes carelessly, mixing up Matt. v. 21, and Matt. xv. 18. See above, c. 20, n. 2.

^10 *teneri.* So Gangn. Gel. and Pam. *teneris*. *teneri* depends on *inquit*.

^11 *et es tam in facto.* Gangn. et *est tam*. Oehl. by conj. *egisti tam*. Gel., Pam. and Rig. *es tam*. Latinius conj. *hæres tam*. The sense is the same. 'You are in (*i. e.* you are convicted in) act as well as thought.

^12 *nec potes leviore.... gravius crimen is facto; levius is cogitatu.* Tertull. has shewn that he who has given such a bond (*cavit*) must have been guilty, *cogitatu*; and has added that this implies guilt, *facto*. 'You are convicted of guilt in act no less than in thought, nor can you

Quicumque fluctus ejus offocant³, omnis vertex⁴ ejus ad inferos desorbet. Nemo dicat, Quis tam tuto præcavebit? <margin>1 Cor. v. 10.</margin> Exeundum de sæculo⁵ erit. <margin>c. XXIV.</margin> perseverent, non observantes renuntient sibi¹⁰. Viderimus enim si secundum arcæ typum¹¹ et corvus et milvus et lupus et canis et serpens in ecclesia erit. Certe idololatres in arcæ typo non habetur. Nullum animal in idololatren figuratum est. Quod in arca non fuit, in ecclesia non sit.

---

¹⁰ *renuntient sibi*, 'let them renounce their faith.'

¹¹ *secundum arcæ typum*. This was a common figure. The raven was the type of an apostate, the kite of a plunderer, the wolf of the cruel, the dog of the unclean. See note in Oxf. Transl. The admission of un-
<column-break/>
clean beasts into the ark furnished an argument for the less rigid disciplinarians. Hippolytus censures Callistus for using this argument, 'Were there not unclean beasts in the ark? Such, therefore, must also be in the church.' Quoted by Bunsen, Hipp. Vol. 1. p. 133.

# TERTULLIANI LIBER
## DE CORONA MILITIS¹.

<margin>
CAP. I.
A Christian soldier has refused to wear a crown. Even so-called Christians have censured him. I will shew the unlawfulness of compliance with this heathen usage.
</margin>
Proxime factum est² liberalitate præstantissimorum imperatorum³; expungebantur⁴ in castris milites laureati. Adhibetur quidam illic magis Dei miles, ceteris constantior fratribus, qui

---

¹ Cod. Agob. **INCIPIT LIBER DE CORONA FELICITER.** Hence Rigalt adopts the title **DE CORONA** for that of **DE CORONA MILITIS**, observing that the subject of this treatise is The use of crowns in general, not only of military crowns. I have retained the fuller title, as being equally appropriate, and having more authority on its side.

Ch. I. ² In the Cod. Ag. (followed by Rig. and Oehl.) the first sentences stand thus: *Proxime factum est : Liberalitas præstantissimorum imperatorum expungebatur in castris, milites laureati adibant. Quidam illic magis Dei miles ceteris constantior fratribus, qui se duobus dominis servire posse præsumpserant, solus libero capite, coronamento in manu otioso, vulgato jam et ista disciplina Christiano, relucebat.* The reading in the text (with certain minor variations) has the authority of the Cod. Magliabechianus and of Rhenan.: *facta est liberalitas* of Pamel, and *facta liberalitate* of Junius, are merely conjectural. In the reading of Rigalt the use of *Proxime factum* may be illustrated by Plaut. Trin. IV. 3, 3: *Jamdudum factum est, cum abiisti domo.*

<column-break/>

³ *imperatorum.* Severus, Caracalla, and Geta. In the year 196 Severus was associated with Albinus and Caracalla. Comp. De Pall. c. 2: *imperii triplex virtus.* In 198 Albinus was defeated and slain. In 200 Geta was associated with his father and brother.

⁴ *expungebantur. expungere* is properly 'to prick off' as in the courts of law, when a case is disposed of. De Virg. Vel. c. 11: *quod supra imtermisimus...nunc responso expungemus.* Apolog. c. 2: *debito pœnæ nocens expungendus est, non eximendus.* Comp. Apolog. c. 44: *qui sententiis elogia dispungitis.* Hence 'to dispose of,' De Idol. c. 1; 'to pay off' as a debt, De Idol. c. 13. Here *expungebantur milites*, 'the soldiers were being paid,' the names of each being pricked off as they received the largess. According to Rigalt's reading *liberalitas expungebatur in castris*, 'the liberality of the Emperors was being carried to completion in the camp.' For this use of *expungere*, 'to complete,' Oehler quotes Ad Natt. I. c. 7: *Hæc cum expunxeris, vives in æternum*; de Orat. c. 9: *quot simul expunguntur officia.*

Denique singuli designare, eludere eminus, infrendere cominus. Murmur tribuno defertur; et persona jam ex ordine accesserat. Statim tribunus, Cur, inquit, tam diversus habitu? Negavit ille cum ceteris sibi licere. Causas expostulatus, Christianus sum, respondit. O militem in Deo gloriosum! Suffragia exinde, et res apud acta⁷, et reus ad præfectos. <margin>Exod. iii. 5.</margin>

⁵ *duobus...* Comp. De Idol. c. 19: *non potest una anima duobus deberi, Deo et Cæsari.* De Spect. c. 26: *nemo enim potest duobus dominis servire.*

⁶ *Vulgata...* ‘Herein the well-known rule of the Christians was publicly shewn forth.’ Or according to Rigalt’s reading, ‘he being now proclaimed to be a Christian by his observance of their well-known rule.’ The use of crowns was repudiated by Christians, because they were used in the service of the heathen gods. See Athen. Lib. xv. Plin. N. H. xxi. 8: *Et jam tunc coronæ Deorum honos erant, et Larium publicorum privatorumque ac sepulcrorum et Manium.* Comp. Arnob. Adv. Gent. vii. p. 237: *Etiamne Dii sertis, coronis, afficiuntur, et floribus?* Lactant. Div. Inst. ii. c. 1: *Ad Deorum templa concurrunt, his libant, his sacrificant, his coronant.* Arnob. p. 151: *Sedent in spectaculis Quindecemviri laureati.* Comp. De Idol. c. 18.

<column-break/>

⁷ *apud acta.* Rig. and Oehl. from Cod. Ag. read *ampliata*, ‘the case was adjourned.’ The reading in the text (which is that of the other MSS. and early Edd.) seems preferable. There is no mention of adjournment. The votes (*suffragia*) are taken, the case is decided and accordingly registered *apud acta* : the accused is brought before the *præfects*, who execute the first part of the sentence by depriving him of military rank.

⁸ *speculatoriam.* Rhen. reads *spiculatoriam*, and Turnebus, Advers. xxvii. 20, supposes a difference between *speculatores* from *spiculum*, and *speculatores* from *speculator*: Torrentius (on Sueton. Cal. c. 52) observes that old inscriptions and MSS. shew that there was but one term *speculator*. The variety of reading sometimes found arises from the common interchange of *i* and *e*. *Speculator* was originally ‘a scout,’ but he was employed as a

Et nunc rufatus$^{11}$ sanguinis sui spe, calceatus de Evangelii paratura, succinctus acutiore verbo Dei, <margin>Eph. vi. 13-17.</margin> totus de Apostolo armatus, et de martyrii candida$^{12}$ melius coronatus, donativum$^{13}$ Christi in carcere exspectat. Exinde sententiæ super illo, nescio an Christianorum (non enim aliæ ethnicorum), ut de abrupto et præcipiti et mori cupido, qui de habitu interrogatus nomini negotium fecerit$^{14}$, solus scilicet fortis, inter tot fratres commilitones solus Christianus. messenger, and in other capacities. The *speculatoria caliga* was the more ordinary kind of military boot, worn by speculators. Sueton. Cal. c. 52: *modo in speculatoria caliga*. De Idol. c. 19: *militia caligata vel inferior quæque*.

$^9$ *gladium... De Idol. c. 19: omnem postea militem Dominus, in Petro exarmando, discinxit. Below, c. 11.*

$^{10}$ *claruit*. Cod. Ag. has ...*ruit*. Rig. *corruit*, and Oehl. *deruit*, both from conjecture. Both these readings involve a change of subject. I have retained the old reading, *claruit*, considering *laurea* as an abl. 'he shone forth with the glory of the laurel, though it was on his hand instead of his brow.'

$^{11}$ *rufatus*. Alluding to the usual color of military cloaks. Mart. xiv. Ep. 129: *Roma magis fuscis vestitur, Gallia rufis, Ille placet pueris, militibusque color.*

$^{12}$ *de martyrii candida* sc. *corona*. La C. quotes Bed. Serm. 18, *De Sanc-*

<column-break/>

tis: *Qui coronam in persecutione purpuream pro passione donavit, ipse in pace viventibus pro justitia meritis dabit candidas.* Some read *coronandus*. Baronius supposes the soldier to have been put to the torture forthwith, and to be now awaiting his death. In these times those who suffered for the faith, though not put to death, were called *martyres*. Comp. De Idol. c. 14, n. 5, and Bunsen's Hippolytus, Vol. 1. p. 137.

$^{13}$ *donativum*. La C. quotes Petr. Damianus: *In talis quippe militiæ arma jurasti, quæ nudos et agiles expetit bellatores: onustos autem atque in Deo segnes a castrorum excubiis arcet, donativi immunes.*

$^{14}$ *qui nomini negotium fecerit*, 'for having brought danger upon the whole body of Christians.' For the use of *nomen*, see De Idol. c. 14, n. 2.

$^{15}$ *prophetias*. The prophecies peculiar to Montanism. See Kaye's Tertull. p. 16—19.

$^{16}$ *tam longam et bonam pacem*.

<margin>CAP. I.</margin>
dubito quosdam Scripturas emigrare<gloss>17</gloss>, sarcinas expedire, fugæ accingi de civitate in civitatem. Nullam enim aliam Evangelio memoriam curant. Novi et pastores eorum in pace leones, in prælio cervos. Sed de quæstionibus confessionum<gloss>18</gloss> alibi docebimus. At nunc quatenus et illud opponunt, Ubi autem prohibemur coronari? hanc magis localem substantiam<gloss>19</gloss> causæ præsentis aggrediar, ut et qui ex sollicitudine ignorantiæ quærunt, instruantur, et qui in defensione delicti contendunt, revincantur, ipsi vel maxime Christiani laureati, quibus in solatium<gloss>20</gloss> quæstio est, quasi aut nullum aut incertum saltem haberi possit delictum quod patiatur quæstionem. Nec nullum autem nec incertum hinc interim ostendam.

<margin>
CAP. II.
<note>
The abstinence from it has the sanction of custom, which may not be gainsayed. This treatise is not for those who cavil, but for those who desire to learn.
</note>
</margin>
Neminem dico fidelium coronam capite nosse alias extra tempus tentationis ejusmodi<gloss>1</gloss>. Omnes

---

The 5th general persecution was under Antoninus, A.D. 167. The 6th under Severus, A.D. 203 : this treatise was written towards the close of the interval of 36 years.

<gloss>17</gloss> *Scripturas emigrare*, ‘are transgressing the Scriptures.’ Dodgs. ‘are removing their Scriptures,’ but there is no authority for such a use of *emigrare*. The quotation *fugæ*, &c. is introduced by way of irony, ‘they are preparing to transgress the Scriptures by a pretended obedience to one text; for this is the only recollection of the Gospel which they care to retain.’

<gloss>18</gloss> *de quæstionibus confessionum*. ‘quibus quæstionibus confessiones martyrum extorquentur.’ <term>Oehl.</term> But *quæstiones* seems rather to bear

<column-break/>

the same meaning as *quæstio* below, ‘upon the inquiries which may arise in respect to confessions of faith.’ These subjects are discussed by Tertull. in the *Scorpiace* and in the *De Fuga in Persecutione*.

<gloss>19</gloss> *magis localem substantiam*. The substantial matter of the present case having its peculiar place here as opposed to the general questions of confessions of faith. *localis* is opposed to *universalis Ad Scapul.* c. 3.

<gloss>20</gloss> *in solatium*. <term>Rig.</term> and <term>Oehl.</term> adopt the reading of Cod. Agob., *quibus id solum quæstio est*, in opposition to the other MSS. and Edd. <term>Oehl.</term> adds the note, *in solum quæstio est non delictum*. But the common reading is surely preferable. ‘Those laureled Christians,

<margin>CAP. II.</margin>
ita observant a catechumenis usque ad confessores et martyras vel negatores<sup>2</sup>. Viderint<sup>3</sup>, unde auctoritas moris, de qua cum maxime<sup>4</sup> quæritur. Porro cum quæritur cur quid observetur, observari interim constat. Ergo nec nullum nec incertum videri potest delictum quod committitur in observationem suo jam nomine vindicandam et satis auctoratam consensus patrocinio. Plane, ut ratio quærenda sit, sed salva observatione, nec in destructionem ejus, sed in ædificationem potius, quo magis ob- <margin>2 Cor. xiii. 10.</margin> serves, cum fueris etiam de ratione securus. Quale est autem, ut tunc quis in quæstionem provocet observationem, cum ab ea excidit? et tunc requirat, unde habuerit observationem, cum ab ea desiit? quando, etsi ideo velit videri ad quæstionem vocare, uti ostendat se non deliquisse in observationis destitutione, nihilominus deliquisse eum con-

who are comforted by the circumstance of there being any inquiry at all, as if anything which admits of an inquiry must be deemed either no crime, or at all events a very uncertain one.'

Ch. II. <sup>1</sup> *capite nosse*. Comp. *Apolog. c. 42: nos coronam naribus novimus*. See below, [c. 5. *alias extra tempus*... 'in other times than those of such a trial as the present.']

<sup>2</sup> *a catechumenis...vel negatores*, 'from the time of their first being catechumens to that of their becoming either confessors and martyrs, or else renegades.'

<sup>3</sup> *Viderint*. Rig. and Cod. Ag. *videris*. The rest *viderint*. 'Let them look to the source whence the authority for this custom is derived.' i. e. 'No matter from what source...'

<column-break/>

This use of *viderint* is common in Tertull. *De Spect. c. 15: viderit... principalis titulus idololatriæ. De Idol. c. 11: viderint, si eædem merces...usui sunt. Ibid. c. 24: viderimus enim si secundum arcæ typum... in ecclesia erit. Apolog. c. 42: viderint qui per capillum odorantur*. Below, c. 13: *Viderint et publici equi. De Præscr. c. 11: viderit qui quærit semper, quia non invenit. De Idol. c. 7: viderit an dictum sit*. Tertullian's views as to the proper limits of inquiry as to articles of faith are laid down in *Præscript. c. 8—12*.

<sup>4</sup> *cum maxime. De Spect. c. 1: qui cum maxime... De Idol. c. 2: de hoc cum maxime suscepta sit et passim*. Rig. here reads *nunc maxime*.

<margin>CAP. III.</margin>
abstinemus. Eucharistiæ sacramentum et in tem-
pore victus et omnibus mandatum a Domino, etiam
antelucanis cœtibus^9 nec de aliorum manu quam
præsidentium^10 sumimus. Oblationes pro defunctis^11,
pro natalitiis^12 annua die facimus. Die dominico

---

ginal mode of celebration was kept up on the anniversary of the *Cœna Domini*. *Concil. Carthag. III. c. 29*: *ut sacramenta altaris non nisi a jejunis hominibus celebrentur, excepto uno die anniversario, quo cœna Domini celebratur*. See Pole's Synops. in Matt. xxvi. 21; Bingham, xv. 7, 6—8. The account of the celebration of the Eucharist in Just. Mar. Apolog. I. p. 97, shews that it was then separate from the love-feast. Comp. Plin. Ep. x. 101, quoted De Spect. c. 26, n. 6.

10 *præsidentium*. Comp. De Idol. c. 7. Just. M. (l. c.) says that the president (ὁ προεστώς) took the bread and wine, and made thanksgiving (εὐχαριστίαν ἐπὶ πολὺ ποιεῖται), and that the deacons distributed the elements to each person present.

11 *Oblationes pro defunctis*. The Eucharistic oblation not for the deliverance from purgatory, but for the enlargement of bliss, and for the merciful judgment of those who rest in the Lord. See the note in Oxf. Translat. Tertull. de Monogam. c. 10: *Enim vero et pro anima ejus orat, et refrigerium interim adpostulat ei, et in prima resurrectione consortium, et offert annuis diebus dormitionis ejus*. Heraldus on Arnob. IV. p. 152, has a long extract from the Eccles. Hierarch. of the Pseudo-Dionysius. This work, which is probably of the 6th century (see Cave's Life of Dionysius), shews the character of the funeral services at

<column-break/>

that time. The writer enters into the question of prayers for the dead; expressly saying that the prayers are only for those who have led a holy life; that they are offered, because it is known that God is willing to perform them; that they are not offered for sinners, and if they were, they would not be granted, agreeably to the passage, 'Ye ask and obtain not, because ye ask amiss.' Originally the bread and wine were an offering of some pious Christians. Cypr. De Op. et El.: *Locuples et dives dominicum quum celebrare te credis, quæ corbanam omnino non respicis; quæ in dominicum sine sacrificio venis; quæ partem de sacrificio, quod pauper obtulit, sumis*. Hence the term *oblatio* was used for, 1 this offering, 2 the prayer with which they were presented, 3 the whole celebration of the Lord's supper. The Eucharist celebrated at a marriage was *oblatio pro matrimonio*. See Neand. Ch. Hist. Eng. Tr. 1. p. 457, foll. Heraldus quotes Cypr. Ep. III. 6: *Denique et dies eorum, quibus excedunt, annotate; ut commemorationes eorum inter memorias martyrum celebrare possimus. Quanquam Tertullus...significet mihi dies, quibus in carcere beati fratres nostri ad immortalitatem gloriosæ mortis exitu transeunt, ut celebrentur hic a nobis oblationes et sacrificia ob commemorationes eorum*. See Bingh. XXIII. 3, 12—17.

12 *natalitiis*. The day of martyrdom was celebrated as the birth-

^13 *Die dominico jejunium.* Comp. *De Idol.* c. 14. See Bingh. XIII. 8, 3. See below, c. 11, n. 9.

^14 *geniculis adorare.* Bingham (l. c.) quotes *Quæst. et Respons. ad Orthodox.* 95, where it is said, on the authority of Irenæus, that Christians prayed kneeling six days in token of the fall, and standing on the Lord's day and in the Pentecostic season in token of the Resurrection, and that this custom originated in apostolic times.

'Or let us suppose that she did establish a law for her own case: then let virgins alone be veiled, and this only when they are going forth to marriage, and not before they have set eyes upon their husbands.' *Cognoverint, sc. oculis. Comp. De Virg. ³ *in ipsa quoque...* 'look in vain for a law in the case of Susanna, or in that of any individual.'

CAP. IV. reperio, sequitur ut traditio consuetudini morem hunc dederit, habiturum quandoque apostoli auctoritatem ex interpretatione rationis⁴. His igitur exemplis renuntiatum erit, posse etiam non scriptam traditionem in observatione defendi, confirmatam consuetudine, idonea teste probatæ tunc traditionis ex perseverantia observationis⁵. Consuetudo autem etiam in civilibus rebus pro lege suscipitur, cum deficit lex, nec differt Scriptura an ratione consistat, quando et legem ratio commendet. Porro si ratione lex constat, lex erit omne jam quod ratione constiterit a quocumque productum⁶. An non putas omni fideli licere concipere et constituere, duntaxat quod Deo congruat, quod disciplinæ conducat, quod saluti proficiat, dicente <margin>Luc. xii. 57.</margin> Domino, *Cur autem non et a vobis ipsis quod justum judicatis?* Ut non de judicio tantum, sed de omni sententia rerum examinandarum dicit et <margin>Phil. iii. 15.</margin> apostolus, *Siquid ignoratis, Deus vobis revelabit,* solitus et ipse consilium subministrare, cum præ<margin>1 Cor. vii. 25. 2 Cor. viii. 8.</margin>ceptum Domini non habebat, et dicere a semetipso,

---

⁴ *habiturum quandoque...* ‘Tradition first established as an usage this custom, which was in after times to have its authority set forth by an apostle, by way of an interpretation of the principle upon which it had been grounded. Tertull. refers to the argument of S. Paul in 1 Cor. xi. 3—15.

⁵ *confirmatam consuetudine.* An unwritten tradition may find its defence in the very fact of its observance, seeing that it has been confirmed by usage, which is a competent witness that the tradition has by that time been sanctioned by continued observance. *Tunc*, at the time of its having passed into a custom.

<column-break/>

⁶ *si ratione...* Even a law owes its obligation to the fact that it is the enunciation of a principle. Therefore any maxim which rests upon a principle, whoever may have laid it down, will have the force of law.

⁷ *sed et ipse.* These words are omitted by Oehler, on the ground that the lacuna in the Cod. Ag. is not sufficient to admit of all the words *a semetipso, sed et ipse*. The omission is not essential to the sense of the passage, and as the other

<margin>
CAP. V.
To wear crowns on the head is unnatural.
</margin>
Major efficitur ratio Christianarum observationum, cum illas etiam natura defendit, quæ prima omnium disciplina est. Ideoque hæc prima præscribit coronam capiti non convenire. Puto autem naturæ Deus noster, qui figuravit hominem et fructibus rerum appetendis, judicandis, consequendis, certos in eo sensus ordinavit per propria membrorum quodammodo organa. Auditum in auribus fodit, visum in oculis accendit, gustum in ore conclusit, odoratum in naribus ventilavit, contactum in manibus æstimavit¹. Per hæc exterioris hominis

---

<columns>2</columns>
MSS. and Edd. retain them, I have left them in the text.
⁸ *Hanc, sc. rationem.* *Ratio* is ‘the reason of the thing,’ the principle upon which an usage rests. *Hanc expostula*, ‘demand the reason of the thing,’ ‘strive to discover the principle of a particular usage.’
⁹ *propterea colenda...* Usage ‘is therefore to be regarded lest (failing usage) there cease to be any interpreter of reason.’ The *ratio* developes itself in usage, so that usage is an *interpres rationis*.
¹⁰ *ut, si et hanc...* ‘in order that if God shall have granted you to

<column-break/>

arrive at this (the reason of the thing) also, you may then learn, not whether any usage ought to be observed, but why.’
¹¹ *sed cur.* The reading of the text is a happy emendation of Oehler. The common reading was *an non observanda sit. Sed cur.* Sed beginning a fresh chapter. Rig. read *non an*, from Cod. Ag., and made the chapter end at *disciplina est*; reading the sentence thus, *non an observanda sit, sed cur major efficitur..... disciplina est.* Gangn. and Pamel. read *exigitur*, but the rest *efficitur*.

CAP. V. ministeria interiori homini administrantia fructus munerum divinorum ad animam deducuntur a sensibus². Quis igitur fructus ex floribus? Substantia enim propria, certe præcipua, coronarum flores agri. Aut odor, inquis, aut color, aut pariter utrumque. Qui erunt sensus coloris, odoris? visus, opinor, et odoratus. Istos sensus quæ membra sortita sunt? oculi, nisi fallor, et nares. Utere itaque floribus visu et odoratu, quorum sensuum fructus est, utere per oculos et nares, quorum sensuum membra sunt³. Substantia tibi a Deo tradita est, habitus a sæculo. Quamquam nec habitus

---

terion of touch in the hands.' Cod. Ag. and two other MSS. read *extimavit*. Rig. (followed by Oehl.) adopts this reading, explaining *extimare*, 'to place in the extremities,' from *extimus*, i. e. *extremus*. Rig. cites from *De Cult. Fœm.* II. 9, in *extimatione temporum*, but there the Cod. Ag. has *existimatione*; the two MSS. which have *extimavit* here, have there *extimationem*, and the rest *æstimationem*. Ducange (in v. *extimare*) tells us that in ecclesiastical writers *extimare* is used for *existimare*, or for *æstimare*. This seems to me conclusive against Rigalt's new interpretation of *extimare*, and in favour of the reading of the text, although *æstimavit* seems a poor expression after *fodit*, *accendit*, etc.

² *Per hæc...* Rig. follows the Cod. Ag. and reads, *Per hæc exterioris hominis administrantia munerum divinorum ab anima deducuntur adsensus.* 'Tertulliani sententia est: per hæc organa, aurium scilicet, oris, oculorum, narium ac denique manuum, quæ omnia exteriori homini, id est, corpori adfamulantur et adminis-

<column-break/>

trant, rerum naturalium, quæ sunt a Deo hominum gratia conditæ, adsensum sive approbationem ab anima deduci. Nempe anima est quæ audit, anima est quæ videt, anima est quæ sentit. Ab anima deducitur vis illa audiendi, videndi, sentiendi. Itaque animæ sentienti recte adsentire dicuntur aures, oculi, manus, per quæ anima audit, videt, tangit.' RIG. This is, as Oehler remarks, an instance of Rigalt's excessive regard to the Cod. Ag. The reading in the text is easy and intelligible. *ministeria*, 'the organs of sense.' Oehl. quotes *Ad Natt.* I. 18: *linguam suam, totum eradicatæ confessionis ministerium. A sensibus ἀπὸ*, not (as Oehl.) *ὑπὸ τῶν αἰσθήσεων. Sensus*, 'the physical sensations,' as *visus, odoratus*, etc. 'Through the instrumentality of the organs of sense, which, being the ministers of the outward man, minister besides to the inner man, the enjoyment of the gifts of God are transmitted from the senses to the soul.'

## CAP. VII.
naturæ de proximo^4 colentes, atque ita velut ex abundanti ceteras quoque rationes dispiciant quæ nostro privatim capiti^5 coronamentis et quidem omnibus interdicunt^6. Nam et urgemur a communione naturalis disciplinæ converti ad proprietatem Christianæ totam jam defendendam per ceteras quoque species coronarum, quæ aliis usibus prospectæ videntur, ut aliis substantiis structæ, ne, quia non ex floribus constant, quorum usum natura signavit, ut ipsa hæc laurea militaris, non credantur admittere sectæ interdictionem, quia evaserint naturæ præscriptionem. Video igitur et curiosius et plenius agendum ab originibus usque ad profectus et excessus rei. Litteræ ad hoc sæculares necessariæ. De suis enim instrumentis sæcularia probari necesse est. Quantulas^7 attigi, credo sufficient. Si fuit aliqua Pandora, quam primam feminarum memorat

---

tus *Deum norunt...de longinquo, non de proximo, necesse est ignorent, etc.* Then in c. 3: *Hac conscientia instructi adversus opinionem ethnicorum convertamur magis ad nostrorum detractatus.*

^4 *de proximo.* All MSS. except Cod. Ag. *proximo*. Cod. Ag. *de proxime*, whence Rig. and Oehl. *de proximo*. This is confirmed by De Spect. c. 2, quoted above.

^5 *nostro privatim capiti*, 'to the head of us Christians peculiarly.' For the constr. of interdicunt, see De Idol. c. 4, n. 2.

^6 *Proinde...interdicunt.* 'Therefore let these crown-lovers own meanwhile, as men, the authority of nature by reason of common wisdom, and as the nearest worshippers of the God of nature, let them own

<column-break/>

the assurances of their own religion; and then let them, over and above, examine the other grounds also, which prohibit crowns and indeed all ornament to our heads in particular.' La Cerda adopts a strange interpretation, making *colentes* govern *homines, propriæ religionis pignora and Deum*, and hence deriving sanction for the worship of saints (*homines*) and relics (*religionis pignora*), as well as of God.

^7 *Quantulas.* 'However slight may be the knowledge which I have attained, it will, I think, be enough for my purpose.' Oehler says, that Tertullian here uses *quantulas*, as elsewhere *quanti* for *quot*. But *quanti* expresses that the number is large, *quantuli* that it is small; e.g. De Idol. c. 8: *Domus vero*

CAP. VII. **privigni.** Hercules nunc populum capite præfert, nunc oleastrum, nunc apium. Habes tragœdiam Cerberi^13, habes Pindarum atque Callimachum, qui et Apollinem memorat interfecto Delphyne^14 dracone lauream induisse, qua supplicem^15. Erant enim supplices coronati^16 apud veteres. Liberum, eundem apud Ægyptios Osirin^17, Harpocration industria hederatum^18 argumentatur, quod hederæ natura sit cerebrum ab heluco^19 defensare. Sed et alias Liberum principem coronæ plane laureæ, in qua ex Indis triumphavit, etiam vulgus agnoscit, cum dies in illum sollemnes Magnam appellat Coronam^20. Si et Leonis Ægyptii^21 scripta volvas, prima Isis repertas spicas capite circumtulit, rem magis ventris. Plura quærentibus omnia exhibebit præstantissimus in hac quoque materia commentator Claudius Sa-

^13 *Habes tragœdiam Cerberi.* These words are omitted in the Cod. Ag., but found in the rest. If they are genuine, we must refer them to the mention of Hercules, *tragœdiam Cerberi* being ‘the tragic account of Cerberus’ defeat.’

^14 *Delphyne.* Oehl. quotes from Suidas, *v.* Δελφοί, Δελφύνη ἢ Δελφίνη. The laurel was especially used by priests. Athen. xv. 13, says that the Carians, in obedience to an oracle of Apollo, only allow willow crowns to be worn in general: τοῖς δὲ θεραπεύουσι τὴν θεὸν ("Ηρην) ἐπιτρέψαι φορεῖν τὸν καὶ νῦν ἔτι διανέμοντα τῆς δάφνης στέφανον.

^15 *qua supplicem.* Soph. Œd. R. 3, ἱκτηρίοις κλάδοισιν ἐξεστεμμένοι, where Brunck interprets ἐξεστεμμένοι, ‘instructi’ non ‘coronati,’ quoting Il. α, 14: στέμματ'

CAP. VII. aliquid sit idolum, sed quoniam quæ idolis alii faciunt ad dæmonas pertinent. Porro si quæ alii idolis faciunt ad dæmonas pertinent, quanto magis quod ipsa sibi idola fecerunt cum adviverent? Ipsi scilicet sibi procuraverant dæmones per eos in quibus esurierant ante quod procuraverant<sup>25</sup>.

<margin>
In the use of things common to all we must make a difference.
What is necessary to life is allowable.
For the rest we must have a Scripture precedent.
</margin>
CAP. VIII. Tene interim hanc fidem<sup>1</sup>, dum incursum quæstionis<sup>2</sup> excutio. Jam enim audio dici et alia multa ab eis prolata quos sæculum deos credidit, tamen et in nostris hodie usibus et in pristinorum sanctorum et in Dei rebus et in ipso Christo deprehendi, non alias scilicet homine functo, quam per communia ista instrumenta exhibitionis humanæ<sup>3</sup>. Plane ita sit, nec antiquius adhuc in originem disceptabo<sup>4</sup>. Primus litteras Mercurius enarraverit<sup>5</sup>: necessarias confitebor et commerciis rerum et nostris erga Deum studiis. Sed etsi nervos idem in sonum strinxit, non negabo et hoc ingenium cum

---

it was invented by Jupiter and the like), and in idols (because it was devised by dæmons acting and dwelling in these persons), while still living. So in *quibus esurierant*.

<sup>25</sup> *Ipsi scilicet...* Tertull. shews why *quod ipsa sibi idola fecerunt* especially belongs to dæmons. In truth (says he) the dæmons had themselves provided these honors for themselves, by means of those in whom they had first thirsted for that which they had provided. *esurire*, 'to desire eagerly,' (as in Eng. 'thirst for'). Rig. quotes Scorp. i. in carcere martyria esuriunt.

Ch. VIII. <sup>1</sup> *hanc fidem.* The faith which forbids crowns on the authority of tradition.

<sup>2</sup> *incursum quæstionis*, sc. intercurrentem quæstionem. A question which intervenes.

<column-break/>

<sup>3</sup> *exhibitionis...* See De Idol. c. 6, n. 2.

<sup>4</sup> *nec antiquius adhuc...* Dodgs. 'Nor will I enquire any further back into the origin of these things.' Tert. hints that the things alluded to were in reality of earlier date, but shews that even allowing this not to be so, they still stand on a different footing to crowns.

<sup>5</sup> *enarraverit.* Cod. Ag. followed by Oehl. *enarravit*, and below *exploravit*. But observe *molita sit*, and comp. above *fecerit* and *fuerit*.

<sup>6</sup> *vestietur.* The weaving of dresses was said to be the invention of Minerva.

<sup>7</sup> *propria Osiridis veste.* Herod.

### CAP.VIII. umbrans corruptelam conditionis, qua subjecta est vanitati⁹. Dicimus enim ea demum et nostris et superioribus usibus et Dei rebus et ipsi Christo competisse quæ meras utilitates et certa subsidia et honesta solatia necessariis necessitatibus vitæ humanæ procurant, ut ab ipso Deo inspirata credantur, priore prospectore et instructore et oblectatore, si forte¹⁰, hominis sui, quæ vero hùnc ordinem excesserunt, ea non convenire usibus nostris, præsertim quæ propterea scilicet nec apud sæculum nec in Dei rebus nec in conversationibus Christi recognosci est.

coronari non oportere. Imagini veritas respondere debebit. Sed forsitan oppones ipsum Christum coronatum. Ad hoc breviter interim audies : Sic et tu coronare, et licitum est. Tamen nec illam impietatis contumeliosæ coronam populus conscivit. Romanorum militum commentum fuit, ex usu rei sæcularis, quem populus Dei nec publicæ unquam lætitiæ nec ingenitæ luxuriæ nomine admisit, facilius cum tympanis et tibiis et psalteriis revertens de captivitate Babyloniæ quam cum coronis et post cibum et potum exsurgens ad ludendum sine coronis. Nam neque lætitiæ descriptio neque luxuriæ denotatio de coronæ decore aut dedecore tacuisset. Adeo et Esaias quoniam inquit³, Cum tympanis et tibiis et psalteriis bibunt vinum, dicturus esset etiam, Cum coronis, si unquam hic usus fuisset et in Dei rebus.

² *dignum Deo... indignum idolo.*
So all the MSS. including Cod. Ag.,
except that the *in* of *indignum* is
effaced. Rig. and Oehl. adopt a
conject. of Ursin., *indignum Deo...
dignum idolo.* This is unnecessary.
Tertull. has said that in order to
have a right to use in common with
the heathen a thing that has been
connected with their false worship,
we must find some instance of its
use in the divine economy. And
why so? Because the two services
differ so widely. 'For what is so
fit for God as that which is unfit
for an idol? What, on the contrary,
so fit for an idol as that which is
fit for the dead also?'

cessat utendi. Cessante natura sentiendi, qua vult⁹, quis abutatur, cum non habeat qua utatur? Nobis autem abuti¹⁰ apud apostolum non licet, facilius¹¹
<margin>1 Cor. vii. 31.</margin>
non uti docentem, nisi quod nec abutuntur qui nihil sentiunt. Sed vacat totum et est ipsum quoque opus mortuum, quantum in idolis, vivum plane, quantum in dæmoniis¹², ad quæ pertinet superstitio.

---

qui careant sensu, and perinde for qui sensu careant and proinde. Cod. Ag. abuti eo vellent. Rig. and Oehl. abuti ea vellent, the rest abuti vellent. For cujus sensu careant atque, Cod. Ag. cujus careant sensu atque. Rhen. cujus sensu carent atque. Gangn., Gel. and Pam. cujus sensu carerent æque, si abuti vellent, si sensu non carerent. Tertullian is referring to the abuse of flowers in wearing them upon the head, as above, c. 5. But since idols and the dead cannot smell them, Tertull. will not call such a custom an abuse, because there is no reality of use; but it is what would be an abuse if they were sentient. Observe the opposition between illorum erit uti, and nobis autem abuti. 'Therefore to such as are without sensation, to them will it belong so to use that, whereof they have no sensation, just as if they would abuse it, if they were not without sensation.'
⁸ Nulla vero distantia. There is no distinction between using and abusing. Here again there is much variety of reading. The text is as in Oehl. The other editions connect cessante natura sentiendi with the sentence preceding it. Gangn., Gel. and Pam. quæve quis abutatur. Rhen. in marg. ed. 1528, qua velut quis. 'But there is no distinction of abusing, when the reality of using fails. Failing the principle of sen-

<column-break/>

sation, by aid of which one chooses to abuse, who can abuse, when he has not wherewith to use?'
⁹ qua vult, 'h. e. cujus ope et auxilio vult abuti, h. e. abusum efficere.' <term>OEHL.</term>
¹⁰ abuti. Gr. καταχρῆσθαι. Oehl. compares De Cult. Fœm. ii. 9: Secundum apostolum tamen, qui nos uti monet mundo isto, quasi non abutamur. La C., overlooking this, is misled by the Vulg. Qui utuntur hoc mundo tanquam non utantur. 'Ait ergo Tertull.: Ita abhorret Paulus ab ipso nomine abusus, ut maluerit dicere non usus, ducens hoc esse facilius potiusque ad docendum.' <term>LA C.</term> Tertull. seems to mean that the apostle's lesson is rather not to use, except that it is more fit to guard the living from abusing, because to abuse is peculiar to them; the dead neither use nor abuse. 'We may not abuse, as the apostle tells us, whose lesson is rather not to use, except that [the living only can abuse] the non-sentient neither use nor abuse.'
¹¹ facilius. 'h. e. aptius, rectius. Ita supr. cap. 7: facilius pudenda foliis quam tempora floribus incinctam describit. cap. 9: facilius cum tympanis et tibiis et psalteriis revertens de captivate Babylonica quem cum coronis.' <term>OEHL.</term>

<gloss>16</gloss> **cum habitu.** Cod. Ag. and Rig. *cum saltitaveris habitu.* All

<column-break/>

the rest as in text. *Saltitaveris* seems quite out of place.

<gloss>17</gloss> **idolis.** Cod. Ag. and Rig. *idolo.* This would make *auctoribus suis* the dat. of the agent, and *suis* for *ejus*; as *De Spect. c. 28, n. 1. But idola* have been shewn to be *auctores*, and *quorum* plainly refers to *auctoribus*, and seems to point out that *auctoribus* are idols. *Hoc* is usually taken as agreeing with *ritu*, ‘with this ceremony and dress and decoration men offer.’ It seems rather to be in the nom. referring to *idolothytum*. The argument is this. What constitutes idolatry is the ceremony and dress and decoration with which an act is performed. For if by a mere word a thing becomes an idol offering, and therefore forbidden, much more when it is defiled by ceremony, dress and decoration. Accordingly a crown is

<margin>CAP. X.</margin>
Hoc idolothytum est, non contigeris, multo magis, cum habitu<sup>16</sup> et ritu et apparatu idolothytorum contaminatur. Ita et corona idolothytum efficitur. Hoc enim ritu et habitu et apparatu idolis<sup>17</sup> immolatur auctoribus suis, quorum eo vel maxime proprius est usus, ne in communionem possit admitti, quod<sup>18</sup> in Dei rebus non invenitur. Propterea apostolus inclamat, Fugite idololatriam. Omnem utique et totam. <margin>1 Cor. x. 14.</margin> Recogita silvam et quantæ latitant spinæ. Nihil dandum idolo, sic nec sumendum ab idolo. <margin>1 Cor. viii. 10.</margin> Si in idolio<sup>19</sup> recumbere alienum est a fide, quid in idoli habitu videri? Quæ communio Christo et Beliæ? <margin>2 Cor. vi. 15.</margin> Et ideo fugite. Longum enim divortium mandat ab idololatria, in nullo proxime agendum. ---

made an idol-offering. For this offering is made by ceremony and dress and decoration to idols who were its inventors. On the use of *immolare*, see De Spect. c. 10, n. 11.

<sup>18</sup> *quod*. There is no comma before *quod* in Rig. or Oehl., and Dodgson interprets ‘that nothing maybe allowed for common use which is not found in the things of God.’ But this would require *inveniatur*. La C. tells us that *quod* is a conjunct. answering to *eo*, ‘to whom its use is peculiar, so that it cannot be admitted to common use, even especially on this account, because it is not found in the divine economy.’

**CAP. X.** Altius Johannes, Filioli, inquit, custodite vos ab idolis! non jam ab idololatria quasi officio, sed ab idolis, id est ab ipsa effigie eorum. Indignum enim, ut imago Dei vivi imago idoli et mortui fias. Usque adhuc proprietatem istius habitus ex originis censu^21 et ex superstitionis usu idolis vindicamus, ex eo præterea, quod dum in rebus Dei non refertur, magis magisque illorum deputatur, in quorum et antiquitatibus et sollemnitatibus et officiis convenitur. Ipsæ denique^22 fores, et ipsæ hostiæ et aræ, ipsi ministri et sacerdotes eorum coronantur. Habes omnium collegiorum sacerdotalium coronas apud Claudium. Sed et illam interstruximus distinctionem differentium rationalium et irrationalium eis occurrentem, qui communionem in omnibus de quorundam exemplorum occasione defendunt.

<margin>Look then to the occasions of crown-wearing. Is the chief cause, military service, allowable? It may not be entered into, but may perhaps, under certain conditions, be continued in,</margin>

statim deserendam esse militiam, quod ex militia, id est per militiæ leges non licet, aut modis omnibus cavendum, ne quid adversus Deum committatur, quod idem ex militiæ Romanæ legibus vix liceat, aut extrema quæque perpetienda pro Deo, quod omnis Christianus Christo fide dicta condixit.' Oehl. *extra militiam*, 'cum nec extra militiam, hoc est in rebus privatis, permittatur fidem semel susceptam deserere, aut cavillari eam et eludere.' Both refer back *quæ permittuntur* to *deserendum* and *cavillandum*. Tertull. means to say that if the converted soldier remains in the army, he must either manage to avoid transgressing God's laws, or he must die for the cause of Christ. The antecedent of *quæ* is to be sought in *ne quid committatur*, 'provided nevertheless that when faith has been embraced and sealed, we must either quit the ser-

<column-break/>

vice, as many have done, or must demur in all ways to doing anything against God's will (which doings are not allowed on the ground of military service more than on any other ground); or in the last place, we must endure for God's sake, what the faith of citizen, no less than soldier, has agreed to suffer.'

<sup>17</sup> *pagana. paganus* opposed to *miles* as we oppose 'citizen' to 'soldier.' *Juv. Sat. xvi. 33: Citius falsum producere testem Contra paganum possis, quam vera loquentem Contra fortunam armati. Paganus* was not used for 'heathen' before the empire became Christian, after which the name was applied to the remote and rude countrymen, as opposed to the more enlightened inhabitants of towns. Herald. Animadv. in Arnob. init. Prudent. adv. Symm. I. 449: *Sint hæc barbaricis gentilia numina pagis.*

<margin>CAP. XI.</margin> sus nomen ejus lucri habitam. Apud hunc tam miles est paganus fidelis, quam paganus est miles fidelis <gloss>18</gloss>. Non admittit status fidei allegationem necessitatis. Nulla est necessitas delinquendi, quibus una est necessitas non delinquendi. Nam et ad sacrificandum et directo negandum necessitate quis premitur tormentorum sive pœnarum <gloss>19</gloss>, tamen nec illi necessitati disciplina connivet, quia potior est necessitas timendæ negationis et obeundi martyrii quam evadendæ passionis et implendi officii. Ceterum subvertit totam substantiam sacramenti causatio ejusmodi, ut etiam voluntariis delictis fibulam laxet <gloss>20</gloss>. Nam et voluntas poterit necessitas

---

<gloss>18</gloss> *miles fidelis*. Gangn., Gel., Pam. and Rig. *miles infidelis*. Dodgs. following Rig. translates: 'In his sight the believer who is no soldier is as much a soldier as the unbeliever who is a soldier is no soldier.' But Cod. Ag., the other MSS., Rhenan. and Oehl. read as in text. 'In his sight the believing citizen is as much a soldier, as the believing soldier is a citizen,' i. e. In his sight there is no difference between soldier and citizen.

<gloss>19</gloss> *Nam et ad sacrificandum*. The argument of necessity may be as well applied to sacrificing and to direct apostasy. There is in that case a constraining necessity of tortures and punishment. Yet this is overborne by the counter necessity of enduring martyrdom for duty's sake. But the whole essence of the baptismal vow is overthrown by an excuse of such a kind, as to loosen the check even upon wilful sins.

<gloss>20</gloss> *fibulam laxet*. Oehl. quotes *De Pudic.* c. 16: *Hujus boni fibulam quis illum nesciat invitum relaxasse,*

<column-break/>

*ut fornicationi obviam esset?* Senec. apud Lactant, I. 16: *Quid ergo est quare apud poetas salacissimus Jupiter desierit liberos tollere? Utrum sexagenarius factus est, et illi lex Papia fibulam imposuit?*

<gloss>21</gloss> *Nam et voluntas...* 'For even the will may be maintained to be necessity, since it admits of being constrained.'

<gloss>22</gloss> *Vel ipse hæc.* Cod. Ag., Rig., Oehl. *ipsam*, sc. necessitatem. Tertull. has reluctantly allowed the possibility of a converted soldier remaining a soldier provided he is ready to suffer martyrdom rather than do an unlawful act, such as crown-wearing. He insists on this condition rejecting the plea of necessity. If there be such necessity, it would only shew that the calling in which it occurs is absolutely unlawful. 'I might,' Tertull. says, 'even myself have advanced these arguments of necessity as a bar not only to military service, but to all other occupations which give occasion to the official use of crowns,

### CAP. XII. Claudius, quamquam et myrto¹ ait milites redimiri solere. Veneris enim myrtus, matris Æneadarum, etiam amiculæ Martis, per Iliam et Romulos Romani². Sed ego Venerem non credo ex hac parte cum Marte Romanam, qua pellicis dolor est³. Cum et olea militia coronatur, ad Minervam est idololatria, armorum æque deam, sed et paci cum Neptuno initæ⁴ ex hac arbore coronatam. In his erit serti militaris superstitio ubique polluta et polluens omnia. Quæ jam polluantur⁵ et causis. Ecce annua votorum nuncupatio⁶ quid videtur? Prima in principiis, secunda in Capitoliis. Accipe post loca et verba: Hunc tibi, Jupiter, bovem cornibus auro decoratis vovemus esse futurum⁷. Cujus sententiæ

---

issue, Tertullian supposes (for the sake of argument), that the preliminary question has been determined in favour of the lawfulness of military service.

Ch. XII. ¹ Plin. N. H. xv. 38: *triumphans de Sabinis Postumius Tubertus in consulatu (qui primus omnium ovans ingressus Urbem est) quoniam rem leviter sine cruore gesserat, myrto Veneris Victricis coronatus incessit, optabilemque arborem etiam hostibus fecit. Hæc postea ovantium fuit corona.*

² *Romani*, ‘by his connexion with Ilia and the Romuli (Romulus and Remus) a Roman.’

³ *Sed ego Venerem...* ‘But I do not suppose that Venus as well as Mars was a Roman by virtue of this connexion, wherein is the resentment occasioned by an intruder.’

⁴ *paci cum Neptuno initæ*. Oehl. refers to Plin. N. H. xvi. 44, and others.

<column-break/>

⁵ *polluantur*. So all the MSS. except Cod. Ag., which has *polluentur*. Oehl. follows La Cerda, who conject. *polluatur*. Tertull. is now proceeding to the discussion of the occasion of crowns, having first shewn them to be in themselves superstitious. ‘On these foundations will rest the superstition of the military garland on every side polluted, and polluting all. Let all moreover be polluted (i. e. be thought to be polluted) through the occasions also on which it is worn.’ Tertull. proceeds to point out the superstition connected with the occasions of wearing the garland.

⁶ *votorum nuncupatio*. This was in the beginning of the year, for the safety of the emperor. Oehl. refers to Sueton. Ner. c. 46: *Votorum nuncupatione magna ordinum frequentia vix repertæ Capitolii claves*. Comp. *Apolog*. c. 28, sqq.

⁷ *vovemus esse futurum*. Ursin.

^8 *donativi*. See above, c. 1.
^9 *Hoc erit...* 'Will *Ye cannot serve God and mammon* be this, to surrender to mammon, and to desert God? Will *Render to Cæsar the things that are Cæsar's, and to God the things that are God's* be this, both not to give up man (which is God's image) to God, and to take the denarius (which is Cæsar's image) from Cæsar? (i. e. in the donative.)' Comp. *De Idol.* c. 15: *Reddite, ait, quæ sunt Cæsaris Cæsari, et quæ sunt Dei Deo, id est imaginem Cæsaris Cæsari, quæ in nummo est, et imaginem Dei Deo, quæ in homine est.*
