CAP. XII. causam^15^, nonne et ipse^16^ pugnavit? Est et alia militia regiarum familiarum^17^. Nam et castrenses^18^ appellantur, munificæ et ipsæ sollemnium Cæsareanorum^19^. Sed et tu^20^ proinde miles ac servus alterius es, et si duorum, Dei et Cæsaris, certe tunc non Cæsaris, cum te Deo debes, etiam in communibus, credo, potiori.

<margin>
**C. XIII.**
magisterial,
triumphal,
provincial,
festal, nuptial, gymnastic, or other.
</margin>

Coronant et publicos ordines laureis publicæ causæ^1^, magistratus vero insuper aureis^2^, ut Athenis, ut Romæ. Superferuntur etiam illis Hetruscæ^3^.

---

*in agris, in castellis, in insulis Christianos.*

^15^ *causam.* Tertull. calls the triumphal crown the cause of the horrors of war, because men fought to gain this honour.

^16^ *et ipse.* He who has worn on his head the cause of 'all this, may be considered himself also to have taken part in the unholy warfare. Oehl. by conj. *et ipsi*, 'has fought even with Christ himself.'

^17^ *regiarum familiarum,* 'There is also another military service in the royal households.' Allusion is made to the various military officers in the emperor's court included under the general name of *aulici*. Among these officers were *Palatini*, *Cortinarii*, *Cæsariani*, *Prætoriani*, *Castrenses*, *Comitatenses*, *Speculatores*, *Stipatores*, *Protectores*, *Cohortales*, *Ministeriani*, *Ostensiones*, *Contubernatus*, *Domestici*.

^18^ *castrenses.* In the old days of Rome the *castrensis corona* was the crown worn by him who first entered the enemy's camp. But under the empire it belonged to the court-officers called *Castrenses*. La Cerda quotes Vopisc. Tacit.: *Paruit prae-*

<column-break/>

*ceptis et voluntati Castrensium ordo nobilissimus.*

^19^ *munificæ et ipsæ...* 'being themselves also officials in the imperial solemnities.' Tertull. calls the crowns (so necessary in all solemnities) a kind of office-bearers in the court. *Munifici* is a proper term for soldiers who bear certain offices (*munia*). Oehl. quotes from Cod. Theodos. xii. 1, 192, *Spontanei hypomnemato-graphi munifici*; and Isidor. Origg. x. 166, *Munificus dictus, vel quia alicui munera dat multa, vel quia munus suum, id est officium quod debet, adimplet.* Observe that *Cæsareanorum* is the adj. to *sollemnium*, and has nothing to do with the *Aulici Cæsareani*.

^20^ *Sed et tu.* Oehl. from conj. *tum*. 'Dicit enim: sed etiam si in hac domus sacræ militia militas, non in altera illa quæ cadaveribus et lacrimis sibi præmia parat, quibus Deum offendat, tamen et in hac sanctum tuum erga Deum officium lædere subinde cogeris.' But *alterius* is 'of another,' i. e. of God. On the use of *alter* for *alius*, see De Spect. c. 14, n. 4. *proinde*. Dodgs. 'henceforth;' more properly for *perinde*. See De Spect.

Sunt et provinciales aureæ$^7$, imaginum jam, non virorum, capita majora$^8$ quærentes. Sed tui ordines et tui magistratus et ipsum curiæ nomen ecclesia est Christi. Illius es, conscriptus in libris vitæ. <margin>Phil. iv. 3.</margin>Illic purpuræ tuæ sanguis Domini et clavus latus in cruce ipsius ; <margin>Matt. iii. 10</margin>illic securis ad caudicem jam arboris posita ; <margin>Es. xi. 1.</margin>illic virga ex radice Jesse. Viderint et

---

c. 2, n. 10. ‘But if they of Cæsar’s household are soldiers, you too are as much a soldier as a servant of another, and if you are a soldier and servant of two persons, of God and Cæsar, (as soldiers must necessarily in a certain sense be), at any rate you are not a servant of Cæsar on those occasions, on which your service is due to God, a master to be preferred, I suppose, even in things indifferent.’

Ch. XIII. $^1$ *publicos ordines... publicæ causæ.* La Cerda quotes Xiphil. in Trajan. : ἔτι δὲ καὶ στεφάνῳ ἐστολισμένον, οἷά που καὶ τὴν γερουσίαν γράφουσι. *Liv.* x. 47: *Eodem anno coronati primum ob res bello bene gestas ludos Romanos spectaverunt.*

$^8$ *capita majora*, ‘requiring now to fit them the larger heads of statues, not of men of ordinary size.’ These crowns, which at first were only marks of honour and of slight weight, became a heavy tax upon the provinces. <margin>C. XIII.</margin>
alienum quomodo sæculum manumittet? Etsi libertas videtur, sed et servitus videbitur. Omnia imaginaria in sæculo et nihil veri. Nam et tunc liber hominis eras, redemptus a Christo, et nunc servus es Christi, licet manumissus ab homine. <margin>1 Cor. vii. 22.</margin> Si veram putes sæculi libertatem, ut et corona eam consignes, redisti in servitutem hominis, quam putas libertatem, amisisti libertatem Christi, quam putas servitutem. Hoc enim superest, ut Olympius Jupiter et Nemæus Hercules et misellus Archemorus et Antinous^14 infelix in Christiano coronentur, ut ipse spectaculum fiat, quod spectare non debeat^15.

<margin>c. XIII.</margin> que censu coronarum<sup>16</sup>, quibus omnia ista redimita sunt. Præfabitur quidem Claudius etiam cœlum sideribus apud Homeri carmina coronatum<sup>17</sup>, certe a Deo, certe homini: igitur et homo ipse a Deo coronandus est. Ceterum a sæculo coronantur et lupanaria<sup>18</sup> et latrinæ et pistrinæ et carcer et ludus<sup>19</sup> et ipsa amphitheatra et ipsa spoliaria<sup>20</sup> ipsæque libitinæ<sup>21</sup>. Quam sacer sanctusque, quam honestus ac mundus sit habitus iste, noli de uno poetico cœlo, sed de totius mundi commerciis æstimare. At enim Christianus nec januam<sup>22</sup> suam laureis infamabit, si norit, quantos deos etiam ostiis diabolus affinxerit, Janum a janua, Limentinum a limine, Forculum et Carnam<sup>23</sup> a foribus et cardinibus, etiam apud Græcos Thyræum Apollinem et Antelios dæmonas.

conject. *spectare tædet.* Flor., Rhen., Gangn., Gel. and Pam. as in text. Vien., Leyd. *debebat.* The Christian by wearing a crown becomes a part of the show, of which he ought not even to be a spectator.

<sup>16</sup> *in solo quoque censu coronarum*, ‘on account of the crowns alone.’ On *census*, see De Idol. c. 4, n. 4.

<sup>17</sup> *coronatum*. Il. Σ. 485: ἐν δὲ τὰ τείρεα πάντα, τά τ᾽ οὐρανὸς ἐστεφάνωται. La Cerda quotes Plin. N. H. xvi. 4: *Antiquitus quidem nulla* (corona) *nisi Deo dabatur.* Ob id Homerus cœlo tantum eas et prælio universo tribuit. The argument of Claudius is this: The heaven is crowned with stars. It was certainly so crowned by God for man’s benefit. Therefore man himself ought to be crowned by God.

<sup>18</sup> *lupanaria*. See De Idol. c. 15, n. 1.

<column-break/>

<sup>19</sup> *ludus*, ‘the gladiatorial school.’ Schools in general were so bedecked. De Idol. c. 10: *flore scholæ coronandæ.*

<sup>20</sup> *spoliaria*, ‘the place where the corpses of gladiators were stripped.’ Lips. (de Amph. c. 21) quotes Lamprid. in Commod.: *Gladiatoris cadaver unco trahatur; gladiatoris cadaver in spoliario ponatur.*

<sup>21</sup> *libitinæ*, ‘the biers of the dead.’ See above, c. 10, n. 3. La Cerda quotes Sidon. Apollin. ii. 8: *Cum libitinam ipsam flentes omnes, externi quoque, prensitarentur, remorarentur, exoscularentur, etc.* The gate of the Spoliarium was called *Porta Libitinensis.*

<sup>22</sup> *januam*. Comp. De Idol. c. 15.

<sup>23</sup> *Carnam*. Cod. Ag. and Rhen. *Carnam.* Gangn., Gelen. and Pamel. *Cardam.* In De Idol. c. 15, we have Cardeam. But Ovid. Fast.

<margin>2 Cor. xi. 2. Eph. v. 23, sqq.</margin>

---

VI. 101: *Prima dies tibi, Carna, datur. Dea cardinis hæc est, Numine clausa aperit, claudit aperta suo.*

Ch. XIV. ¹ *Tanto abest*, for *tantum abest. De Test. Anim. c. 1: Tanto abest, ut nostris litteris annuant homines. De Fug. in Persec. c. 2: tanto abest, ut eam ex proprietate possideat.* Oehl. supposes an ellipse of *magis*. But the abl. seems to mark the interval, as *tanto distat*, rather than to imply any comparison of distance. 'So far,' not 'so much farther.' On the ellipse of *magis*, see De Spectac. c. 16, n. 8.

² *humilitatis suæ sarcinam.* 1 Cor. xi. 8—10 is discussed in *De Orat. c. 22*, and *De Virg. Vel. c. 7*. In the latter *ἐξουσίαν* is rendered *potestatem* without further comment. La Cerda remarks that *sarcina* implies the same as *ἐξουσία*, or *potes-*

<column-break/>

*tas, 'quæ scilicet imponitur supra caput, habetque veluti potestatem cogendi fœminam ad servandam modestiam, dejiciendum caput, submittendos oculos.'* **De Virg. Vel. c. 17:** *Aliæ modice linteolis, credo ne caput premant, nec ad aures usque demissis, cerebro tenus operiuntur.* The veil is called by Tertull. *jugum subjectionis*, by Chrysost. *insigne subjectionis*, by *Conc. Gangr. memoria subjectionis. Illos coronatos*, alluding to Rev. iv. 4, 'much more if her head is crowned will she give offence to those who are perhaps at that very moment crowned in a different manner.'

³ *Quid enim...quam*, sc. *quid aliud quam. De Idol. c. 20: quid erit dejeratio quam. De Virg. Vel. c. 11: nihil primum senserunt quam erubescendum. Apul. Met. IV. 6: nec juxta quidquam, quam.*

<vocab>Tertullian, De Corona Militis, idolatry, Christ, headship, veiling, modesty, sarcina, exousia, crown of thorns</vocab>

<columns>2</columns>
⁴ *centenariis*. The ancient reading was *centaureis*. Rhenan. by conj. *centenariis*, followed by Gangn., Gel. and Pamel. La Cerda by conj. *spontaneis*, which he defends by the Gr. αὐτόματα, in Herod. l. c., and remarks upon the passages adduced in support of *centenariis*. *Centenariis* is however found in Cod. Ag. Herod. viii. 138: οἱ δὲ ἀπικόμενοι ἐς ἄλλην γαῖαν τῆς Μακεδονίης οἴκησαν πέλας τῶν κήπων τῶν λεγομένων εἶναι Μίδεω τοῦ Γορδίεω, ἐν τοῖς φύεται αὐτόματα ῥόδα, ἓν ἕκαστον ἔχον ἑξήκοντα φύλλα, ὀδμῇ τε ὑπερφέροντα τῶν ἄλλων. Theophr. De Plant. vi. 6: ἑκατοντάφυλλα ῥόδα. Plin. N. H. xxi. 10: *Cæpio Tiberii Cæsaris principatu negavit centifoliam (rosam) in coro-*

<column-break/>

nas addi, præterquam extremos velut ad cardines.
⁵ *utrisque liliis*. Plin. N. H. xxi. 11, mentions *alba lilia* and *rubens lilium quod Græci crinon vocant*. Gr. λείριον and κρίνον. Athen. xv. 27: τὰ δὲ κρίνα φησὶν ὁ Θεόφραστος εἶναι καὶ πορφυρανθῆ. Φιλῖνος δὲ τὸ κρίνον ὑφ’ ὧν μὲν λείριον, ὑφ’ ὧν δὲ ἴον καλεῖσθαι.
⁶ *omnibus violis*, sc. purpureis, luteis, albis. Plin. N. H. xxi. 14.
⁷ *favos post fella*. Proverbial.
Ch. XV. ¹ *prato. Sedent.* So Cod. Ag., Rig. and Oehl. The rest *Protosedent*, or *proto sedent*. Ursin. by conj. *Porro sedent*. Rig. explains *cælesti prato* as a synonym of the rainbow, ‘ob speciem smaragdi instar viridantem.’ Gr. καὶ ἶρις

CAP. XV.
Christ will hereafter crown his saints, as he crowns his angels. Look for no other crown but that which is eternal in the heavens.
Apoc. ii. 7, 10.
2 Tim. iv. 7, 8.
Apoc. vi. 2.
Apoc. x. 1.
Apoc. iv. 4.
Apoc. xiv. 14.
</margin>
Serva Deo rem suam intaminatam. Ille eam,
si volet, coronabit. Immo et vult denique, invitat.
Qui vicerit, inquit, dabo ei coronam vitæ. Esto et
tu fidelis ad mortem et decerta et tu bonum ago-
nem, cujus coronam et apostolus repositam sibi
merito confidit. Accipit et angelus victoriæ coro-
nam procedens in candido equo, ut vinceret, et
alius iridis ambitu ornatur cœlesti prato¹. Sedent
et presbyteri coronati eodemque auro et ipse Filius
hominis supra nubem micat. Si tales imagines
in visione, quales veritates in repræsentatione! Illas
aspice, illas odora. Quid caput strophiolo² aut
dracontario³ damnas, diademati destinatum? Nam
et reges nos Deo et Patri suo fecit Christus Jesus. <margin>Apoc. v. 10.</margin>
Quid tibi cum flore morituro? Habes florem⁴ ex virga <margin>Es. xi. 1.</margin>

¹ <language>Greek</language>κυκλύθεν τοῦ θρόνου ὁμοία ὁράσει σμαραγδίνῳ.<language>Latin</language>

² *strophiolo.* Plin. N. H. XXI. 2: *Tenuioribus utebantur antiqui, stropos appellantes, unde nata strophiola.*

³ *dracontario.* <language>English</language>Dracontaria were circlets of gold in the form of serpents worn on the neck and arms and sometimes (as Tertull. implies) on the head.<language>Latin</language> Herald. (Digress. I. 23), first pointed this out, quoting Lucian, Amor. c. 41: <language>Greek</language>τοὺς περὶ καρποῖς καὶ βραχίοσι δράκοντας, ὡς ὤφελον ὄντως ἀντὶ χρυσίου δράκοντες εἶναι.<language>Latin</language> La Cerda quotes from Clem. Alex. Pædag. II. c. ult.: <language>Greek</language>ἤδη δὲ καὶ φανερώτατα τοῦ πονηροῦ σύμβολα οὐκ αἰσχύνονται περικείμεναι. ὡς γὰρ τὴν Εὔαν ὁ ὄφις ἠπάτησεν,<language>Latin</language>

<column-break/>

<language>Greek</language>οὕτω δὲ καὶ τὰς ἄλλας γυναῖκας ὁ κόσμος ὁ χρυσοῦς, δελέατι προσχρώμενος τοῦ ὄφεως τῷ σχήματι, ἐξέμηνεν εἰς ὕβρεις, σμυραίνας τινὰς καὶ ὄφεις ἀποπλαττομένας εἰς εὐπρέπειαν. λέγει γοῦν ὁ κωμικὸς Νικόστρατος ἀλύσεις, καθετῆρας, δακτυλίους, βουβάλια, ὄφεις, περισκελίδας, ἐλλέβορον·<language>Latin</language> and Hieronym. ad Marcell.: *Cum primum hoc propositum arripuit, aurum colli sui, quod quidem Murænulam vulgus vocat, (quod scilicet metallo in virgulas lentescente quadam ordinis flexuosi catena contexitur) absque parentibus vendidit.*

⁴ *florem.* Es. xi, 1, LXX.: <language>Greek</language>καὶ ἐξελεύσεται ῥάβδος ἐκ τῆς ῥίζης Ἰεσσαὶ, καὶ ἄνθος ἐκ τῆς ῥίζης ἀναβήσεται.<language>Latin</language>

<header>TERTULLIANI LIBER *DE CORONA MILITIS*.</header>

## CAP. XV.
Jesse, super quem tota Divini Spiritus gratia requievit, florem incorruptum, immarcescibilem, sempiternum, quem et bonus miles eligendo in cœlesti dignatione, ordinatione profecit⁵. Erubescite, Romani commilitones ejus, jam non ab ipso judicandi, sed ab aliquo Mithræ⁶ milite, qui cum initiatur in spelæo, in castris vere tenebrarum, coronam interposito gladio sibi oblatam, quasi mimum martyrii, dehinc capiti suo accommodatam monetur obvia manu a capite pellere, et in humerum, si forte, transferre, dicens Mithram esse coronam suam. Atque exinde nunquam coronatur, idque in signum habet ad probationem sui, sicubi tentatus fuerit de sacramento, statimque creditur Mithræ miles, si dejecerit coronam, si eam in deo suo esse dixerit. Agnoscamus ingenia diaboli, idcirco quædam de divinis affectantis, ut nos de suorum fide confundat et judicet.

---

⁵ *in cœlesti dignatione, ordinatione profecit*, 'has been promoted in heavenly honour and rank.' The asyndeton is common in Tertull. Cod. Ag. (followed by Oehl.) omits *dignatione*. Rig. reads *in cœlesti ordinatione dignatione profecit*, translated by Dodgs.: 'hath been promoted to honour in the ranks of heaven.'

⁶ *Mithræ*. The Persians worshipped the sun under the name of Mithras. See Bähr on Herod. i. 131, and Barthius on Stat. Theb. i. 719: *seu Persei sub rupibus antri Indignata sequi torquentem cornua Mithram*. Herod. mentions Μίτρα

<column-break/>

as a synonym of Venus; and some suppose that the Persians worshipped the sun as Mithras, and the moon as Mithra. The word Mithras constantly forms part of Persian proper names, as *Mithridates, Mitrobates, Ithamitres, Siromitres*, and the like. Comp. *De Præscr.* c. 40: *Et si adhuc memini, Mithra signat illic in frontibus milites suos ; celebrat panis oblationem, et imaginem resurrectionis inducit, et sub gladio redimit coronam. Adv. Marc.* i. c. 13: *sicut aridæ et ardentis naturæ sacramenta leones Mithræ philosophantur.*

<vocab>Tertullianus, De Corona Militis, Mithras, Jesse, Spiritus Divinus, Mithrae miles, martyrium, Herodotus</vocab>

# The Cambridge Bible for Schools and Colleges.

## GENERAL EDITOR: J. J. S. PEROWNE, D.D., DEAN OF PETERBOROUGH.

> “It is difficult to commend too highly this excellent series.” — *Guardian.*

> “The modesty of the general title of this series has, we believe, led many to misunderstand its character and underrate its value. The books are well suited for study in the upper forms of our best schools, but not the less are they adapted to the wants of all Bible students who are not specialists. We doubt, indeed, whether any of the numerous popular commentaries recently issued in this country will be found more serviceable for general use.” — *Academy.*

> “Of great value. The whole series of comments for schools is highly esteemed by students capable of forming a judgment. The books are scholarly without being pretentious: information is so given as to be easily understood.” — *Sword and Trowel.*

### Now Ready. Cloth, Extra Fcap. 8vo.
